{"parentUuid":null,"isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":"RESEARCH ONLY — do not write to any repository file. You are researching for Volyume, a deterministic (no-AI) strength/hypertrophy coaching app, to design a LIVE SET PRESCRIPTION resolver: \"given everything the app legitimately knows before this set, what load and rep target should it suggest now?\" Your output feeds a binding design document, so evidence quality and honest uncertainty labelling matter more than volume.\n\nUse web search against primary scientific literature (PubMed/journals: J Strength Cond Res, Sports Med, Eur J Appl Physiol, PeerJ, Front Physiol; authors like Helms, Schoenfeld, Zourdos, Steele, Fisher, Greig Nuckols' reviews are acceptable secondary anchors when they cite primaries). For EVERY claim, attach a citation (first author, year, journal) and classify it as one of exactly: SUPPORTED (direct experimental/meta-analytic evidence), REASONABLE COACHING INFERENCE (consistent with evidence, standard practice, not directly tested), UNSUPPORTED / DO NOT CLAIM.\n\nResearch questions (answer each explicitly):\n1. Progressive overload modalities: rep progression vs load progression vs double progression vs mixed — what does the evidence actually show about their relative effectiveness for hypertrophy/strength? Is double progression (fill the rep range, then add load) evidence-based or convention?\n2. Is there evidence that EVERY set must reach the top of a rep range before load increases, vs aggregate/majority/first-set criteria? What load increment sizes are typical/supported (% vs absolute)?\n3. Within-session fatigue: what is the typical rep drop-off across successive sets at fixed load and fixed rest (numbers, ranges, moderators like rest length, exercise size, training status)? Cite studies quantifying rep decrement across 3-5 sets.\n4. Autoregulation using CURRENT-session performance (velocity-based training's logic, APRE — autoregulatory progressive resistance exercise): what supports adjusting later-set targets from earlier-set performance TODAY? What does APRE literature actually prescribe and what were outcomes vs fixed loading?\n5. Day-to-day performance variability in trained lifters: how large is normal fluctuation in reps-at-load or e1RM (numbers if available)? Implication: how much should one below-par set move a prescription?\n6. Single prior session as a prescription basis: what's known about reliability of one session as an estimate of current ability vs a small rolling window (2-4 sessions)?\n7. Set-position/order effects: evidence that later sets/exercises underperform due to accumulated fatigue and that this is somewhat stable within an individual?\n8. RIR/RPE: what can and cannot be inferred WITHOUT per-set RIR collection (Volyume only has an optional whole-SESSION difficulty rating 1-5 and actual load×reps). Accuracy of RIR self-report near/far from failure (Zourdos, Hackett). We will NOT invent per-set RIR — establish what the session-level rating can legitimately gate.\n9. Proximity to failure and hypertrophy: current meta-evidence (e.g. Robinson et al. 2023/2024) on RIR vs hypertrophy/strength — does aiming for +1 rep at same load beat +load at same reps for practical prescriptions?\n10. Practical equipment increments: standard plate/dumbbell/machine-stack increments and microloading — what granularity is realistic (2.5kg plates, 1-2kg dumbbell steps, 5kg+ stacks)?\n\nOutput format: numbered sections matching the questions; each with 3-8 bullet findings, each bullet = claim + citation + classification tag. End with a 10-line \"what this licenses the resolver to do / forbids it from claiming\" summary. No fluff, no generic essay."},"uuid":"1f64c7b2-2a07-4a34-bc26-22377fbd7b32","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:52:11.522Z","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

{"parentUuid":"2ecfc568-4f11-496e-bea6-b081ab3c8513","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","attachment":{"type":"skill_listing","content":"- session-start-hook: Creating and developing startup hooks for Claude Code on the web. Use when the user wants to set up a repository for Claude Code on the web, create a SessionStart hook to ensure their project can run tests and linters during web sessions.\n- marketing-claims-check: Use to compliance-check any piece of text or file against Volyume's CLAIMS-STANDARDS before it is published or reused — e.g. \"check this claim\", \"is this copy compliant\", \"review this draft for compliance\". Works on ad hoc text, not just content produced by the marketing skills.\n- marketing-draft: Use to produce a single Volyume marketing content item on demand, given a channel and topic (e.g. \"draft a blog article on progressive overload\" or \"write an Instagram caption about the food diary\"). Runs content-writer then the compliance gate and stages or flags the result.\n- marketing-ship-web: Use to publish approved web content (articles/pages) to volyume.app — the AUTONOMOUS-lane web publishing path from OPERATING-CHARTER §4. Use after an artefact has a compliance PASS on record and is ready to go live, or when the marketing-weekly cycle reaches its publish step for web items.\n- marketing-weekly: Use to run the full Volyume Marketing HQ weekly cycle (OPERATING-CHARTER §3) — monitor, analyse, produce content, gate for compliance, design, stage, publish the autonomous lane, ledger, and founder digest. Use once per week, or on explicit request to run the weekly cycle now.\n- dataviz: Use this skill whenever you are about to create ANY chart, graph, plot, dashboard, or data visualization, in ANY output medium — an HTML or React artifact, inline SVG, plotting code in any library (matplotlib, plotly, d3, Recharts, …), an image/PNG you will render and upload, or a chart shared into Slack. Read it BEFORE writing the first line of chart code, choosing chart colors, building a stat tile / meter / KPI row, or laying out a dashboard. Produces visualizations that read as one system — elegant, accessible, consistent in light and dark — using a brand-neutral placeholder palette you swap for your own. Teaches a design-system-agnostic method: a form heuristic, a color formula with a runnable validator, mark specs, and interaction rules. A validated default palette is documented in `references/palette.md` — swap that file's values for your brand's. Triggers on: \"chart\", \"graph\", \"plot\", \"data viz\", \"visualization\", \"dashboard\", \"analytics\", \"visualize data\", \"categorical colors\", \"sequential / diverging palette\", \"stat tile\", \"sparkline\", \"heatmap\", \"legend\", \"axis\", \"tooltip\", \"chart colors\", \"color by series\".\n- artifact-design: Design guidance and fundamentals for Artifacts. - Load before writing any artifact, including Markdown ones — format is part of the design pass, never a speed shortcut.\n- artifact-diagramming: Diagramming know-how for Artifacts — when a picture earns its place, how to draw one that shows the real mechanism, and the inline-SVG mechanics that keep it legible in both themes.\n- artifact-capabilities: Runtime capabilities a published Artifact page can be granted — behavior static HTML cannot provide on its own, such as the page reading live or connected data, keeping state shared across viewers, handing the viewer a file to save, or updating and republishing itself. Serves this user's live capability roster and the typed call definitions. Load it whenever the user asks for an artifact needing any such runtime behavior.\n- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- code-review: Review the current diff, or a PR number/branch/path target, for correctness bugs and reuse/simplification/efficiency cleanups at the given effort level (low/medium: fewer, high-confidence findings; high→max: broader coverage, may include uncertain findings); with no level given, it reuses the level you typed last. Pass --comment to post findings as inline PR comments, or --fix to apply the findings to the working tree after the review.\n- simplify: Review the changed code for reuse, simplification, efficiency, and altitude cleanups, then apply the fixes. Quality only — it does not hunt for bugs; use /code-review for that.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- claude-api: Reference for the Claude API / Anthropic SDK — model ids, pricing, params, streaming, tool use, MCP, agents, caching, token counting, model migration.\nTRIGGER — read BEFORE opening the target file; don't skip because it \"looks like a one-liner\" — whenever: the prompt names Claude/Anthropic in any form (Claude, Anthropic, Fable, Opus, Sonnet, Haiku, `anthropic`, `@anthropic-ai`, `claude-*`, `us.anthropic.*`, `[1m]`); the user asks about an LLM (pricing/model choice/limits/caching) — never answer from memory; OR the task is LLM-shaped with provider unstated (agent/MCP/tool-definition/multi-agent/RAG/LLM-judge/computer-use; generate/summarize/extract/classify/rewrite/converse over NL; debugging refusals/cutoffs/streaming/tool-calls/tokens).\nSKIP only when another provider is being worked on (overrides all triggers): OpenAI/GPT/Gemini/Llama/Mistral/Cohere/Ollama named in the query; OR `grep -rE 'openai|langchain_openai|google.generativeai|genai|mistralai|cohere|ollama'` over the project hits (run this grep FIRST if no provider named — don't Read the file).\n- run: Launch and drive this project's app to see a change working. Use when asked to run, start, or screenshot the app, or to confirm a change works in the real app (not just tests). First looks for a project skill that already covers launching the app; otherwise falls back to built-in patterns per project type (CLI, server, TUI, Electron, browser-driven, library).\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch\n- docx: Use this skill whenever the user wants to create, read, edit, or manipulate Word documents (.docx files) or Word templates (.dotx files). Triggers include: any mention of 'Word doc', 'word document', '.docx', '.dotx', or requests to produce professional documents with formatting like tables of contents, headings, page numbers, or letterheads. Also use when extracting or reorganizing content from .docx or .dotx files, inserting or replacing images in documents, performing find-and-replace in Word files, working with tracked changes or comments, or converting content into a polished Word document. If the user asks for a 'report', 'memo', 'letter', 'template', or similar deliverable as a Word or .docx file, use this skill. Do NOT use for PDFs, spreadsheets, Google Docs, or general coding tasks unrelated to document generation.\n- morning: Render the user's morning brief as a styled HTML artifact, or set it up as a recurring weekday task. Use only when the user explicitly asks to run, see, or set up their morning brief, or if they invoke /morning by name. A question about their day, schedule, or calendar is not by itself a request for the brief; answer it directly instead.\n- pdf: Use this skill whenever the user wants to do anything with PDF files. This includes reading or extracting text/tables from PDFs, combining or merging multiple PDFs into one, splitting PDFs apart, rotating pages, adding watermarks, creating new PDFs, filling PDF forms, encrypting/decrypting PDFs, extracting images, and OCR on scanned PDFs to make them searchable. If the user mentions a .pdf file or asks to produce one, use this skill.\n- pptx: Use this skill any time a .pptx or .potx file is involved in any way — as input, output, or both. This includes: creating slide decks, pitch decks, or presentations; reading, parsing, or extracting text from any .pptx or .potx file (even if the extracted content will be used elsewhere, like in an email or summary); editing, modifying, or updating existing presentations; combining or splitting slide files; working with templates (.potx), layouts, speaker notes, or comments. Trigger whenever the user mentions \"deck,\" \"slides,\" \"presentation,\" or references a .pptx or .potx filename, regardless of what they plan to do with the content afterward. If a .pptx or .potx file needs to be opened, created, or touched, use this skill.\n- skill-creator: Create new skills, modify and improve existing skills, and measure skill performance. Use when users want to create a skill from scratch, edit, or optimize an existing skill, run evals to test a skill, benchmark skill performance with variance analysis, or optimize a skill's description for better triggering accuracy.\n- xlsx: Use this skill any time a spreadsheet file is the primary input or output. This means any task where the user wants to: open, read, edit, or fix an existing .xlsx, .xlsm, .xltx, .csv, or .tsv file (e.g., adding columns, computing formulas, formatting, charting, cleaning messy data); create a new spreadsheet from scratch or from other data sources; or convert between tabular file formats. Trigger especially when the user references a spreadsheet file by name or path — even casually (like \"the xlsx in my downloads\") — and wants something done to it or produced from it. Also trigger for cleaning or restructuring messy tabular data files (malformed rows, misplaced headers, junk data) into proper spreadsheets. The deliverable must be a spreadsheet file. The effects of load or repetition progression on muscular adaptations - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36199287/\"},{\"title\":\"Progressive overload without progressing load? The effects of load or repetition progression on muscular adaptations\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9528903/\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) Progressive Overload Affects the Magnitude of Muscle Hypertrophy\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/400404759_Progressive_Overload_Affects_the_Magnitude_of_Muscle_Hypertrophy\"},{\"title\":\"PeerJ Progressive overload without progressing load? The effects of load or repetition progression on muscular adaptations\",\"url\":\"https://peerj.com/articles/14142/\"},{\"title\":\"Progressive Overload: 5 Proven Methods to Get Stronger\",\"url\":\"https://www.hypro.app/blog/progressive-overload-guide\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of resistance training with controlled versus self-selected repetition duration on muscle mass and strength in untrained men\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7061915/\"},{\"title\":\"Impact of high versus low fixed loads and non-linear training loads on muscle hypertrophy, strength and force development\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4899398/\"}]\n\nI found research on this topic. Here are the key findings from randomized trials comparing different progression strategies:\n\n## Key Study: Load vs. Repetition Progression\n\nA randomized trial compared two resistance training programs: increasing load while keeping repetition range constant versus increasing repetitions while keeping load constant. The study included 43 resistance-trained participants over an 8-week period.\n\n## Results:\n\nRectus femoris muscle growth modestly favored the repetition progression group. However, dynamic strength increases slightly favored the load progression group, with differences of questionable practical significance. No other notable between-group differences were found across outcomes like other muscle thicknesses, endurance, countermovement jump, body fat, or leg lean mass.\n\n## Practical Conclusion:\n\nBoth progressions of repetitions and load appear to be viable strategies for enhancing muscular adaptations over an 8-week training cycle.\n\nThe research suggests that while load progression provides a slight advantage for strength, and repetition progression shows a modest edge for muscle growth, both methods are effective approaches to progressive overload with minimal practical differences between them in an 8-week timeframe.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"46d06eb2-f6de-47ab-8d61-56aa3dd7825d","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:52:22.803Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"8644b948-16da-4373-83cd-f3f611eee2a3","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

Mechanical Variables During the Deadlift Exercise\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3916933/\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here are the key findings regarding repetition drop-off across successive sets with fixed loads and rest intervals in trained men:\n\n## Performance Decline Across Sets\n\nPerformance declines across successive sets due to neuromuscular fatigue, and this decline is modulated by rest interval duration.\n\n## Set-Specific Decline Patterns\n\nFor the 1-minute rest condition, performance reductions (relative to the first set) were observed commencing with the second set; whereas for the other conditions (2, 3, and 5 minutes rest), performance reductions were not evident until the third and fourth sets. This was observed in a study of sixteen trained men who performed 5 consecutive sets of the bench press with an absolute 3RM load and 1, 2, 3, or 5 minutes of rest interval between sets.\n\n## Quantified Load Reductions Needed\n\nResearch has quantified the specific load reductions needed to maintain repetition performance: 15% load reductions were necessary to maintain performance of 10 repetitions in 3 successive sets of back squat and leg curls separated by 1-minute rests in recreationally trained men. More specifically, during a squat protocol with 1-minute rest intervals, men needed a 15% reduction in load for each set in succession (to maintain repetition number), whereas women needed only a 10% reduction.\n\n## Overall Effect Summary\n\nShort rest periods (≤60 s) appreciably reduce the number of repetitions performed across multiple sets compared to longer rest durations.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"4e8596c5-344d-44d7-819d-d6b2b2e52cd3","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:52:24.378Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"3de7297a-54de-4f6d-b713-d7a89ae88906","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34346831/\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of autoregulatory progressive resistance exercise periodization versus linear periodization on muscular strength and anaerobic power in collegiate wrestlers\",\"url\":\"https://minds.wisconsin.edu/handle/1793/78483\"},{\"title\":\"Utilization of autoregulatory progressive resistance exercise in transitional rehabilitation periodization of a high school football-player following anterior cruciate ligament reconstruction: a case report - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25328831/\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, I found information about the Mann 2010 study comparing APRE to linear periodization:\n\n## Study Overview\n\nThe study examined whether 6 weeks of autoregulatory progressive resistance exercise (APRE) was more effective at improving strength compared with traditional linear periodization (LP) in Division I College football players. The research compared 23 division 1 collegiate football players (2.65 +/- 0.8 training years) who were trained using either APRE (n = 12) or LP (n = 11) during 6 weeks of preseason training in 2 separate years.\n\n## Key Differences Between Methods\n\nAPRE is a method by which athletes increase strength by progressing at their own pace based on daily and weekly variations in performance, unlike traditional linear periodization (LP), where there is a set increase in intensity from week to week.\n\n## Strength Outcomes\n\nAutoregulatory progressive resistance exercise demonstrated greater improvement in 1RM bench press strength (APRE: 93.4 +/- 103 N vs. LP: -0.40 +/- 49.6 N; ANCOVA: F = 7.1, p = 0.02), estimated 1RM squat strength (APRE: 192.7 +/- 199 N vs. LP: 37.2 +/- 155 N; ANOVA: F = 4.1, p = 0.05) and the number of repetitions performed at a weight of 225 lb (APRE: 3.17 +/- 2.86 vs. LP: -0.09 +/- 2.40 repetitions; ANCOVA: F = 6.8, p = 0.02) compared with the LP group over the 6-week training period.\n\nThe study concluded that APRE was superior to traditional linear periodization for improving strength outcomes in college athletes over the 6-week period.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"16c5a585-df08-470f-b1eb-57260dc8112b","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:52:37.784Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"9ddda873-0cbf-4bfe-9289-2d030f227939","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

{"parentUuid":"2b935aba-4d28-43a8-a9cc-82bedd4c0c69","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Yc6JZxbGFBfKsE8g4to695","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"velocity-based training vs percentage-based training meta-analysis strength hypertrophy 2023\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"The effects of velocity-based vs. percentage-based resistance training on sports performance in trainedindividuals: a systematic review and meta-analysis | BMC Sports Science, Medicine and Rehabilitation | Springer Nature Link\",\"url\":\"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s13102-025-01504-9\"},{\"title\":\"Comparison of velocity-based versus percentage-based resistance training on maximum strength, power, jumping, linear sprinting, and change of direction speed in athletes and non-athletes: A systematic review and meta-analysis - Recep Soslu, Ismail Can Çuvalcıoğlu, Hüseyin Şahin Uysal, Abdullah Uysal, Rohit Kumar Thapa, Mustafa Şakir Akgül, Amador Garcia-Ramos, 2026\",\"url\":\"https://doi.org/10.1177/17479541261452541\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of velocity based training vs. traditional 1RM percentage-based training on improving strength, jump, linear sprint and change of direction speed performance: A Systematic review with meta-analysis\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8601436/\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of velocity based training vs. traditional 1RM percentage-based training on improving strength, jump, linear sprint and change of direction speed performance: A Systematic review with meta-analysis | PLOS One\",\"url\":\"https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pone.0259790\"},{\"title\":\"Full article: Comparison of the effects of velocity-based vs. traditional resistance training methods on adaptations in strength, power, and sprint speed: A systematic review, meta-analysis, and quality of evidence appraisal\",\"url\":\"https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/02640414.2022.2059320\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of velocity based training vs. traditional 1RM percentage-based training on improving strength, jump, linear sprint and change of direction speed performance: A Systematic review with meta-analysis - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34793506/\"},{\"title\":\"Frontiers in Physiology (Aug 2022)\",\"url\":\"https://doaj.org/article/e51fe9e21a994bc7acf5842fcfbb993d\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what recent meta-analyses have found comparing velocity-based training (VBT) and percentage-based training (PBT):\n\n## Overall Findings\n\nVBT produced greater improvements than traditional strength training in strength (ES = 0.26, p = 0.03), power (ES = 0.30, p = 0.02), and jumping performance (ES = 0.36, p = 0.01), according to a 2026 meta-analysis.\n\nHowever, the results for strength specifically are more nuanced. The differences in training effects between VBT and PBT were not significant in back squat 1RM, according to an earlier meta-analysis.\n\n## Mechanisms of Adaptation\n\nResearch suggests the two approaches may work through different mechanisms: VBT should produce a better stimulus for neural adaptations owing to higher repetition velocity and lower training stress, while it was likely for PBT to produce better adaptation in muscle morphology due to higher mechanical and metabolic stress.\n\n## Hypertrophy\n\nThe search results don't provide specific meta-analytic conclusions about hypertrophy outcomes comparing the two methods. The findings focus primarily on strength, power, and athletic performance measures like jumping and sprinting rather than muscle growth specifically.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"fba7228a-5d56-47b5-833f-0b777ec69b79","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:52:50.450Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"2b935aba-4d28-43a8-a9cc-82bedd4c0c69","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

{"parentUuid":"82787c42-dfc1-44dc-b181-db1834cad711","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Wtrtd68vyfP1k3zoXawJNp","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Robinson 2024 proximity to failure repetitions in reserve meta-analysis hypertrophy strength dose-response\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"The Failure Factor: The Relationship Between Proximity to Failure and Strength and Hypertrophy | Biolayne\",\"url\":\"https://biolayne.com/reps/issue-32/the-failure-factor-the-relationship-between-proximity-to-failure-and-strength-and-hypertrophy/\"},{\"title\":\"Exploring the Dose-Response Relationship Between Estimated Resistance Training Proximity to Failure, Strength Gain, and Muscle Hypertrophy: A Series of Meta-Regressions - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38970765/\"},{\"title\":\"Exploring the dose-response relationship between estimated resistance training proximity to failure, strength gain, and muscle hypertrophy: a series of meta-regressions - Abertay University\",\"url\":\"https://rke.abertay.ac.uk/en/publications/exploring-the-dose-response-relationship-between-estimated-resist/\"},{\"title\":\"Exploring the Dose-Response Relationship Between Estimated Resistance Training Proximity to Failure, Strength Gain, and Muscle Hypertrophy : A Series of Meta-Regressions | SportRxiv\",\"url\":\"https://sportrxiv.org/index.php/server/preprint/view/295\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) Exploring the Dose-Response Relationship Between Estimated Resistance Training Proximity to Failure, Strength Gain, and Muscle Hypertrophy: A Series of Meta-Regressions\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/370837310_Exploring_the_Dose-Response_Relationship_Between_Estimated_Resistance_Training_Proximity_to_Failure_Strength_Gain_and_Muscle_Hypertrophy_A_Series_of_Meta-Regressions\"},{\"title\":\"Training to Failure vs Reps in Reserve: What Research Shows | FitCraft\",\"url\":\"https://getfitcraft.com/science/training-to-failure-vs-reps-in-reserve\"},{\"title\":\"Full article: Similar muscle hypertrophy following eight weeks of resistance training to momentary muscular failure or with repetitions-in-reserve in resistance-trained individuals\",\"url\":\"https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/02640414.2024.2321021\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, I found information about the Robinson 2024 study on proximity to failure and its effects on strength and hypertrophy:\n\n## Study Overview\n\nThe study quantified proximity to failure as the number of repetitions in reserve (RIR) and meta-regressed 55 hypertrophy and 67 strength studies.\n\n## Key Findings\n\nThe dose-response relationship between proximity to failure and strength gain appears to differ from the relationship with muscle hypertrophy, with only the latter being meaningfully influenced by RIR. More specifically:\n\n- **Hypertrophy**: Muscle hypertrophy significantly increases as training sets are performed closer to failure.\n\n- **Strength**: Strength gains showed negligible differences with different proximities to failure.\n\n## Publication Details\n\nThe study was published in Sports Medicine in 2024 (vol. 54, no. 9, pp. 2209-2231) and was authored by Robinson ZP and colleagues including Pelland JC, Remmert JF, Refalo MC, Jukic I, Steele J, and Zourdos MC.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"96263989-70ff-4d24-b632-9996646a8aaa","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:53:00.559Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"82787c42-dfc1-44dc-b181-db1834cad711","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}
{"parentUuid":"08e3d695-ec29-400d-bf8f-dd48fd8a43e0","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01LP4Rmft1ThCiDJSsFkvFLX","type":"tool_result","content":"# Study Summary: Progressive Overload Without Progressing Load\n\n**Full Author List & Publication:**\nPlotkin, Coleman, Van Every, Maldonado, Oberlin, Israetel, Feather, Alto, Vigotsky, and Schoenfeld (2022), PeerJ\n\n**Design Details:**\n- **Sample:** n=43 resistance-trained participants (38 completed; 27M, 16F)\n- **Training Status:** ≥1 year consistent lower body resistance training\n- **Duration:** 8 weeks\n- **Frequency:** 2 sessions per week (non-consecutive days)\n- **Volume:** 4 sets of 4 exercises (back squat, leg extension, straight-leg calf raise, seated calf raise)\n- **Initial Rep Range:** 8-12 repetitions maximum\n\n**Progression Protocols:**\n\n*LOAD Group (n=22):* Increased load while maintaining 8-12 rep target. Started with 10RM testing to establish initial loads; increments unspecified in available details.\n\n*REPS Group (n=21):* Increased repetitions while maintaining constant load. Participants added reps across training sessions without weight increases.\n\nBoth groups trained to momentary concentric failure with controlled tempos (1s concentric, 2s eccentric).\n\n**Key Results:**\n\nMuscle thickness (ultrasound): Most sites showed negligible differences. Rectus femoris sum favored REPS with \"2.8 mm adjusted effect (CI90%, −0.5 to 5.8 mm).\"\n\n1RM strength: \"Slightly favored LOAD (2.0 kg [−2.4, 7.8]),\" representing approximately 10% greater gains.\n\nLeg extension endurance: Nearly identical between groups (1.02× multiplicative effect).\n\n**Authors' Conclusion:**\n\"Both progressions of repetitions and load appear to be viable strategies for enhancing muscular adaptations over an 8-week training cycle.\""}]},"uuid":"3732d140-0fe3-4dce-b981-5ef70e06b6ee","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:53:02.207Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"08e3d695-ec29-400d-bf8f-dd48fd8a43e0","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

How To Perfect Your Ability To Predict Repetitions In Reserve.\",\"url\":\"https://www.strongerbyscience.com/reps-in-reserve/\"},{\"title\":\"Novel Resistance Training–Specific Rating of Perceived Exertion Scale Measuring Repetitions in Reserve | Semantic Scholar\",\"url\":\"https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/Novel-Resistance-Training%E2%80%93Specific-Rating-of-Scale-Zourdos-Klemp/b5d70ba1d961dd2085545fcec54cfffeea6fcf49\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) Novel Resistance Training-Specific RPE Scale Measuring Repetitions in Reserve\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/277891585_Novel_Resistance_Training-Specific_RPE_Scale_Measuring_Repetitions_in_Reserve\"},{\"title\":\"Novel Resistance Training–Specific Rating of Perceived Exertion Scale Measuring Repetitions in Reserve\",\"url\":\"https://www.ovid.com/jnls/nsca-jscr/abstract/10.1519/jsc.0000000000001049~novel-resistance-trainingspecific-rating-of-perceived?redirectionsource=fulltextview\"},{\"title\":\"Application of the Repetitions in Reserve-Based Rating of Perceived Exertion Scale for Resistance Training\",\"url\":\"https://journals.lww.com/nsca-scj/fulltext/2016/08000/application_of_the_repetitions_in_reserve_based.10.aspx\"},{\"title\":\"A Weight-Dependent 1RM Prediction Equation Optimized on 303,494 Near-Failure Sets Across 388 Exercises\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.17495\"},{\"title\":\"Novel Resistance Training–Specific Rating of Perceived... : The Journal of Strength & Conditioning Research\",\"url\":\"https://journals.lww.com/nsca-jscr/fulltext/2016/01000/novel_resistance_training_specific_rating_of.31.aspx\"},{\"title\":\"Exercise type, training load, velocity loss threshold, and sets affect the relationship between lifting velocity and perceived repetitions in reserve in strength-trained individuals - PMC\",\"url\":\"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12360324/\"},{\"title\":\"RPE Measuring Repetitions in Reserve\",\"url\":\"https://openrepository.aut.ac.nz/server/api/core/bitstreams/16ad9fb9-5da8-446b-9cd9-aae75afb0b92/content\"}]\n\nI found information about the Zourdos et al. 2016 study. Here are the key findings:\n\n## Study Overview\n\nThe primary aim of the study was to compare rating of perceived exertion (RPE) values measuring repetitions in reserve (RIR) at particular intensities of 1 repetition maximum (RM) in experienced (ES) and novice squatters (NS).\n\n## Methodology\n\nTwenty-nine individuals (24.0 ± 3.4 years) performed a 1RM squat followed by a single repetition with loads corresponding to 60, 75, and 90% of 1RM and an 8-repetition set at 70% 1RM. Average velocity was recorded at 60, 75, and 90% 1RM and on the first and last repetitions of the 8-repetition set.\n\n## Key Findings\n\nThey found strong inverse correlations between movement velocity and RPE for both experienced (r = -0.88) and novice (r = -0.77) participants, supporting the scale's construct validity.\n\nAdditionally, the standard deviation of the scores reported for single repetitions at 100, 90, 75, and 60% of 1RM were 0.32, 0.92, 0.97, and 1.18, respectively, for the experienced squatters, indicating that experienced lifters demonstrated greater accuracy with the RPE scale at higher intensities.\n\nThe results demonstrated the use of an RIR-based RPE scale could be a viable method to not only quantify effort during resistance training but also to regulate training load in real-time, offering a practical way to adjust intensity based on the lifter's perceived capacity to perform additional repetitions.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"4e1365cb-89b1-4f30-9eb4-5ecde65a2ce6","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:53:15.371Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"cce1ac32-70b8-4df5-b6b7-5c8fa8fb241c","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

Zourdos\n\n**Number of Studies:** The abstract does not specify the exact number of studies included for hypertrophy or strength analyses separately.\n\n**RIR Range Examined:** The abstract does not explicitly state the range of repetitions in reserve (RIR) values examined.\n\n**Effect Estimates and Confidence Intervals:**\n\nFor **strength outcomes**: \"the confidence intervals of the marginal slopes for estimated RIR contained a null point estimate, indicating a negligible relationship with strength gains.\"\n\nFor **muscle hypertrophy outcomes**: \"the marginal slopes for estimated RIR were negative and their confidence intervals did not contain a null point estimate, indicating that changes in muscle size increased as sets were terminated closer to failure.\"\n\nThe abstract concludes that proximity to failure differentially affects these outcomes, with hypertrophy improving closer to failure while strength gains remain similar across varying RIR levels. {"parentUuid":"60c90b36-f8dd-4251-94f1-56aa877103a2","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01QFEZwLgAYqJdbXi66RLwz8","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Hackett accuracy predicting repetitions to failure bodybuilders underestimate reps remaining\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Accuracy in Estimating Repetitions to Failure During Resistance Exercise - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27787474/\"},{\"title\":\"Overshooting, Undershooting, Or Just Right? How To Perfect Your Ability To Predict Repetitions In Reserve.\",\"url\":\"https://www.strongerbyscience.com/reps-in-reserve/\"},{\"title\":\"Estimation of Repetitions to Failure for Monitoring... : The Journal of Strength & Conditioning Research\",\"url\":\"https://journals.lww.com/nsca-jscr/fulltext/2018/05000/Effects_of_Inspiratory_Muscle_Training_on.22.aspx\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) Ability to predict repetitions to momentary failure is not perfectly accurate, though improves with resistance training experience\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/321395253_Ability_to_predict_repetitions_to_momentary_failure_is_not_perfectly_accurate_though_improves_with_resistance_training_experience\"},{\"title\":\"A Weight-Dependent 1RM Prediction Equation Optimized on 303,494 Near-Failure Sets Across 388 Exercises\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.17495\"},{\"title\":\"What Weight Should I Be Using? [Estimated Reps To Failure A Guide to Optimal Training] - MTP Health\",\"url\":\"https://www.mtphealth.com.au/blog/what-weight-should-i-be-using-estimated-reps-to-failure-a-guide-to-optimal-training\"},{\"title\":\"RPE and RIR: The Complete Guide – MASS Research Review\",\"url\":\"https://massresearchreview.com/2023/05/22/rpe-and-rir-the-complete-guide/\"},{\"title\":\"Accuracy in predicting repetitions to task failure in ...\",\"url\":\"https://sportrxiv.org/index.php/server/preprint/download/67/101/84\"},{\"title\":\"Ability to predict repetitions to momentary failure is not perfectly accurate, though improves with resistance training experience\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5712461/\"},{\"title\":\"Training to Failure vs Reps in Reserve: What Research Shows | FitCraft\",\"url\":\"https://getfitcraft.com/science/training-to-failure-vs-reps-in-reserve\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what research has found about accuracy in predicting repetitions to failure:\n\n## Key Findings from Hackett Research\n\nHackett and colleagues conducted studies assessing accuracy in estimation of repetitions to failure (ERF) during resistance exercise. Their findings revealed important patterns in how people predict their remaining repetitions:\n\n**General Underestimation Pattern:**\nParticipants generally underpredicted the number of repetitions to task failure by approximately one rep on average (0.95 reps to be exact), indicating a tendency toward underestimation. When someone thinks they only have two reps left in the tank, they likely still have about three reps in the tank, on average.\n\n**Accuracy by Proximity to Failure:**\nSignificant differences were found for error of ERF among actual repetitions to failure (ARF), with the error of ERF ∼1 repetition at ARF 0-5 compared with >2 repetitions at ARF 7-10. This means accuracy improves as you get closer to actual failure.\n\n**Additional Factors:**\nGreater accuracy was found for the chest press compared with leg press, with the error of ERF ≤1 repetition for ARF 0-5 and ARF 0-3, respectively. Resistance training experience did not affect the accuracy in ERF.\n\n**Bodybuilder-Specific Findings:**\nThe bodybuilders estimated their reps left until failure with pretty good accuracy.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"275f28bc-c0f4-48b0-8a2d-3d41fc97a114","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:53:27.802Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"60c90b36-f8dd-4251-94f1-56aa877103a2","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27494056/\"},{\"title\":\"A Weight-Dependent 1RM Prediction Equation Optimized on 303,494 Near-Failure Sets Across 388 Exercises\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.17495\"},{\"title\":\"Validity and reliability of upper body push and pull tests to determine one-repetition maximum\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10343053/\"}]\n\nI've performed the web search for your query on test-retest reliability of repetitions to failure at fixed percentage 1RM with coefficient of variation measurements.\n\nThe search returned several relevant studies on 1RM reliability testing. Here are the key findings:\n\n**General 1RM Test-Retest Reliability:**\n\nTest-retest intraclass correlations (ICCs) ranged from 0.64 to 0.99 (median ICC = 0.97), where 92% of ICCs were ≥ 0.90, and coefficients of variation (CVs) ranged from 0.5 to 12.1% (median CV = 4.2%).\n\n**Specific to Repetitions to Failure Methods:**\n\nWhen examining 1RM estimations using maximal repetitions to failure methods, intraclass correlation coefficient was > 0.96, and coefficient of variation (CV) was in the range 2.3–8.3% for all tests.\n\n**Exercise-Specific Considerations:**\n\nThe relationship between repetitions to failure and percentage of 1RM is not uniform across exercises, and exercise type was the only moderator that meaningfully affected the repetitions–%1RM relationship, with the relationship best described by natural cubic splines rather than linear forms.\n\nThe research indicates that test-retest reliability for repetitions-to-failure protocols at fixed 1RM percentages is generally excellent, with favorable coefficient of variation values.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"ea7f4123-d28f-48c4-991b-0569d7bde109","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:53:38.022Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"dbc6c576-c61c-41cf-a683-4dbaec2d9999","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

| BMC Sports Science, Medicine and Rehabilitation | Springer Nature Link\",\"url\":\"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s13102-025-01137-y\"},{\"title\":\"The Validity and Reliability of the Individual Load–Velocity Profiles in the Deadlift and Hip Thrust Exercises in Men and Women\",\"url\":\"https://www.mdpi.com/2076-3417/16/4/2109\"},{\"title\":\"Predicting 1RM strength with velocity-based training – Human Kinetics\",\"url\":\"https://us.humankinetics.com/blogs/excerpt/predicting-1rm-strength-with-velocity-based-training\"},{\"title\":\"Efficacy of daily one-repetition maximum training in well-trained powerlifters and weightlifters: A case series | Request PDF\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/305525911_Efficacy_of_daily_one-repetition_maximum_training_in_well-trained_powerlifters_and_weightlifters_A_case_series\"},{\"title\":\"Evaluation of the Vmaxpro sensor for assessing movement velocity and load-velocity variables: accuracy and implications for practical use\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10765425/\"},{\"title\":\"Velocity-Based Training\",\"url\":\"https://www.scienceforsport.com/velocity-based-training/\"},{\"title\":\"The Utility of Angular Velocity During Back Squat to Predict 1RM and Load–Velocity Profiling\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12526633/\"},{\"title\":\"A Novel Approach to 1RM Prediction Using the Load-Velocity Profile: A Comparison of Models\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8309813/\"}]\n\nI've found relevant research on day-to-day variability in velocity measurements and load-velocity profiles for trained lifters. Here are the key findings:\n\n## Coefficients of Variation\n\nCoefficients of variation for velocity measurements ranged from 1.86 to 6.72% in intra-day testing, while coefficients of variation for intra- and inter-day reliability ranged from 2.4% to 9.7% and from 3.2% to 8.6% respectively across different exercises and measurement conditions.\n\n## Day-to-Day Variability Issues\n\nThe research highlights an important practical concern: Large day-to-day variability in velocity at submaximal loads can produce large differences in the slopes of these lines, and the estimated 1RM strength. Additionally, day-to-day fluctuations in strength have been shown to be as large as 18% above and below the previously tested 1RM.\n\n## Load-Velocity Profile Reliability\n\nLoad-velocity profiles demonstrated excellent concurrent validity (r = 0.97–0.99; standard error of the estimate = 0.03–0.06 m·s−1) and high test–retest reliability (ICC = 0.97–0.99; coefficient of variation < 5%) in deadlift and hip thrust exercises.\n\n## Practical Implications\n\nDay-to-day variability in performance does not account for physiological and psychological stressors that might affect an individual's day-to-day resistance training performance, which is an important consideration when using load-velocity profiles for training prescription.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"484a9021-0c73-4f7b-8141-07b1a27ceabc","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:53:38.596Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"36d0c29a-c8fa-4ca6-8589-097a01f01a9c","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

{"parentUuid":"f44cf9e1-91e2-40d3-9f6c-5aaa1570bc90","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_015G5xVPa7eEWc2xaqzXfo8Y","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"exercise order effect resistance training repetitions performed later exercises fatigue systematic review\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Exercise Order in Resistance Training | Sports Medicine | Springer Nature Link\",\"url\":\"https://link.springer.com/article/10.2165/11597240-000000000-00000\"},{\"title\":\"Influence of Exercise Order on the Number of Repetitions,... : The Journal of Strength & Conditioning Research\",\"url\":\"https://journals.lww.com/nsca-jscr/_layouts/15/oaks.journals/downloadpdf.aspx?an=00124278-201303000-00030\"},{\"title\":\"Comparative effect of order based resistance exercises on number of repetitions, rating of perceived exertion and muscle damage biomarkers in men - ScienceDirect\",\"url\":\"https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S1888754615000490\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of Resistance Exercise Order on the Number of Repetitions Performed to Failure and Perceived Exertion in Untrained Young Males - PMC\",\"url\":\"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3916922/\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) What influence does resistance exercise order have on muscular strength gains and muscle hypertrophy? A systematic review and meta-analysis\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/339365541_What_influence_does_resistance_exercise_order_have_on_muscular_strength_gains_and_muscle_hypertrophy_A_systematic_review_and_meta-analysis\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) What influence does resistance exercise order have on muscular strength gains and muscle hypertrophy? A systematic review and meta-analysis\",\"url\":\"https://www.academia.edu/51615911/What_influence_does_resistance_exercise_order_have_on_muscular_strength_gains_and_muscle_hypertrophy_A_systematic_review_and_meta_analysis\"},{\"title\":\"Exercise order in resistance training - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22292516/\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) Influence of Exercise Order in a Resistance‐Training Exercise Session\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/7273328_Influence_of_Exercise_Order_in_a_Resistance-Training_Exercise_Session\"}]\n\nI've found relevant research on exercise order effects in resistance training. Here are the key findings from the search results:\n\n## Main Effects on Repetition Performance\n\nExercise order affects repetition performance over multiple sets, with the total repetitions and volume being greater when an exercise is placed at the beginning of a resistance training session, regardless of the relative amount of muscle mass involved.\n\nMulti-joint and single-joint exercises performance were negatively impacted when performed later in sequence. Specifically, one study found that a multi-joint exercise's total mean repetitions decreased by 33.6% when preceded by other exercises, while a single-joint exercise's repetitions decreased by 42.3% when performed later.\n\n## Mechanisms Related to Fatigue\n\nAccumulated fatigue from preceding exercises in a sequence increased systematically along the rest intervals after successive exercises. The metabolic recovery ability, reflected in oxygen consumption during rest intervals, indicates the accumulated fatigue and skeletal muscle's ability to recover from the anaerobic exercise performed.\n\n## Important Caveats\n\nHowever, one study found that different exercise order across sequences did not affect the number of repetitions completed to failure in younger populations, suggesting that age and metabolic recovery capacity may influence these effects.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"613ab16e-6188-46de-8527-c8db889c8554","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:53:51.679Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"f44cf9e1-91e2-40d3-9f6c-5aaa1570bc90","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}
{"parentUuid":"72129876-cc0f-4384-8f13-23b43bbc48f9","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01YQEWeJS2fjefy65WsZfAvh","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"\"daily\" 1RM fluctuation \"18%\" strength day to day variation velocity based training rationale\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Velocity-Based Training Options for Strength - SimpliFaster\",\"url\":\"https://simplifaster.com/articles/velocity-based-training-options-strength/\"},{\"title\":\"VBT: Velocity Based Training\",\"url\":\"https://stuartbauld.wixsite.com/performance/single-post/2019/10/20/vbt-velocity-based-training\"},{\"title\":\"Velocity-Based Training\",\"url\":\"https://www.scienceforsport.com/velocity-based-training/\"},{\"title\":\"Velocity Based Training: theory and application - GymAware\",\"url\":\"https://gymaware.com/velocity-based-training/\"},{\"title\":\"Velocity Based Training Zones Explained - GymAware\",\"url\":\"https://gymaware.com/velocity_zones/\"},{\"title\":\"‘Force Velocity Strength Training’ in the ACL rehabilitation. - Physioblog by Andreas Bjerregaard\",\"url\":\"https://andreasphysioblog.com/?p=3637\"},{\"title\":\"Predicting 1RM strength with velocity-based training – Human Kinetics\",\"url\":\"https://us.humankinetics.com/blogs/excerpt/predicting-1rm-strength-with-velocity-based-training\"},{\"title\":\"Variability in the 1 RM in athletes - Vitruve\",\"url\":\"https://vitruve.fit/blog/variability-in-the-1-rm-in-athletes-vitruve/\"},{\"title\":\"Practical considerations for Force Velocity strength training in rehabilitation - Physioblog by Andreas Bjerregaard\",\"url\":\"https://andreasphysioblog.com/?p=3116\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, I found comprehensive information about daily 1RM fluctuation and the rationale for velocity-based training:\n\n## Daily 1RM Fluctuation\n\nResearch by Mladen Jovanovic and Eamon Flannagan suggests that 1RM strength can vary by 18% in either direction on any given day. Day-to-day fluctuations in strength can be as large as 18% above and below the previously tested 1RM, equating to a total variance of 36%.\n\n## Causes of Daily Variation\n\nAn athlete's 1RM can fluctuate by approximately 18% on a daily basis due to normal biological variability of the nervous system caused by training-related fatigue or lifestyle factors such as sleep, stress and nutrition.\n\n## Impact on Percentage-Based Training\n\nThis variability creates significant problems for traditional percentage-based programming. If an athlete is to work at an intensity of 80%1RM for a given day, due to the current readiness of their nervous system at that precise moment in time, the load could actually be as high as 98%1RM. For example, those athletes who were prescribed a load of 70% of 1RM for training may in actuality be lifting in a range of 52%–88% of 1RM.\n\n## Velocity-Based Training Rationale\n\nThe absolute load an athlete can lift (or their 1RM) can fluctuate daily whereas velocity has been shown to be relatively stable regardless of the state of readiness of the nervous system. Velocity-based training allows the coach to adjust the training to fit the freshness of the athlete because strength and 'freshness/readiness' of an athlete changes on a daily basis.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"e8057caf-f1dc-4637-bb23-0dcaa6939920","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:53:53.438Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"72129876-cc0f-4384-8f13-23b43bbc48f9","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

{"parentUuid":"25f4d29d-4361-428a-aac6-0cd08526c92b","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01F6MYtrFy1ezmLrNyDVSw5F","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Willardson Burkett rest interval length repetitions sustained squat bench press sets 1 2 3 number of repetitions decline\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Effect of Rest Interval Length on the Volume Completed During Upper Body Resistance Exercise - PMC\",\"url\":\"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3763284/\"},{\"title\":\"Effect of rest interval length on repeated 1 repetition maximum back squats - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/14636099/\"},{\"title\":\"The Effects of Rest Interval Length on Acute Bench Press ...\",\"url\":\"https://paulogentil.com/pdf/The%20Effects%20of%20Rest%20Interval%20Length%20on%20Acute%20Bench%20Press%20Performance%20-%20The%20Influence%20of%20Gender%20and%20Muscle%20Strength.pdf\"},{\"title\":\"The effect of rest interval length on the sustainability of squat and bench press repetitions - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16686571/\"},{\"title\":\"THE EFFECT OF REST INTERVAL LENGTH ON BENCH PRESS... : Journal of Strength and Conditioning Research\",\"url\":\"https://www.ovid.com/jnls/nsca-jscr/abstract/00124278-200605000-00027~the-effect-of-rest-interval-length-on-bench-press\"},{\"title\":\"The Effect of Rest Interval Length on the Sustainability of Squat and Bench Press Repetitions | Request PDF\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/7093905_The_Effect_of_Rest_Interval_Length_on_the_Sustainability_of_Squat_and_Bench_Press_Repetitions\"},{\"title\":\"International Journal of Human Sciences ISSN:2458-9489\",\"url\":\"https://j-humansciences.com/index.php/IJHS/article/download/4865/2378/18245\"},{\"title\":\"Why Resting Between Sets Is Crucial for Strength Training\",\"url\":\"https://vitalperformancecare.com/rest-periods/\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, I found information about Willardson and Burkett's research on rest intervals in resistance training:\n\n## Key Findings:\n\n**Four Sets Study (8RM Load):**\nWillardson and Burkett (2005) compared repetition performance when completing four sets of the back squat and bench press with an 8-RM load and one, two, or five minute rest intervals. For the back squat, the total repetitions progressively increased as the rest interval increased: one minute (22.47 ± 4.79), two minutes (25.53 ± 4.29), and five minutes (28.80 ± 3.08). The same results were demonstrated for the bench press: one minute (17.13 ± 4.42), two minutes (21.60 ± 4.52), and five minutes (25.73 ± 4.23).\n\n**Five Sets Study (15RM Load - Sustainability):**\nThe study compared the effect of 3 different rest intervals on the sustainability of squat and bench press repetitions over 5 consecutive sets performed with a 15 repetition maximum (RM)-load. During each testing session, 5 consecutive sets of the squat and the bench press were performed with a 30-second, 1-minute, or 2-minute rest interval between sets. For each exercise, significant declines in repetitions occurred between the first and the fifth sets.\n\n**Key Finding on Performance Decline:**\nWhen performing 5 sets of 15 repetitions, neither 30-second, 1-, or 2-minute rest intervals were sufficient to maintain performance, and 3-minute rest interval was more effective (i.e., higher repetitions performed) than 2- and 1-minute rest intervals for maintaining bench press performance.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"b4f036ec-3a57-4b3b-b109-c63bbc03df39","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:54:07.368Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"25f4d29d-4361-428a-aac6-0cd08526c92b","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30988859/\"},{\"title\":\"Without fail: Muscular adaptations in single set resistance ...\",\"url\":\"https://sportrxiv.org/index.php/server/preprint/download/484/1032/967\"},{\"title\":\"Fatigue and Metabolic Responses during Repeated Sets of Bench Press Exercise to Exhaustion at Different Ranges of Motion\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11057609/\"},{\"title\":\"Relationship between maximal oxygen uptake, within-set fatigue and between-set recovery during resistance exercise in resistance-trained men and women\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10863198/\"},{\"title\":\"Predicting Total Back Squat Repetitions from Repetition Velocity and Velocity Loss\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10203840/\"},{\"title\":\"Frontiers | Effect of resistance training programs differing in set structure on muscular hypertrophy and performance in untrained young men\",\"url\":\"https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/physiology/articles/10.3389/fphys.2023.1301535/full\"}]\n\nBased on my search results, I found relevant information about repetitions performed across successive sets at 70% 1RM with 2-minute rest intervals in trained men.\n\nOne study with resistance trained men performed 5 sets of bench press exercise at 70% 1RM with maximal number of repetitions in each and with 5 minutes rest periods between each set, which is similar to your query parameters but with longer rest.\n\nMore directly relevant, another study with ten resistance trained men performed three sets to momentary failure with two-min rest intervals. {"parentUuid":"f74c06d4-8202-4e28-9bb1-204cf8147d8c","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_015W9e6WmYdhKtbpagFFz68q","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"session RPE resistance training validity Day 2004 Foster monitoring internal load whole session\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"(PDF) Session-RPE Method for Training Load Monitoring: Validity, Ecological Usefulness, and Influencing Factors\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/320801144_Session-RPE_Method_for_Training_Load_Monitoring_Validity_Ecological_Usefulness_and_Influencing_Factors\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) Validity and reliability of the session RPE method for monitoring exercise training intensity\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/279439907_Validity_and_reliability_of_the_session_RPE_method_for_monitoring_exercise_training_intensity\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) Session-RPE Method for Training Load Monitoring: Validity, Ecological Usefulness, and Influencing Factors\",\"url\":\"https://www.academia.edu/82745688/Session_RPE_Method_for_Training_Load_Monitoring_Validity_Ecological_Usefulness_and_Influencing_Factors\"},{\"title\":\"Validity of Session Rating Perceived Exertion Method for Quantifying Internal Training Load during High-Intensity Functional Training\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6162408/\"},{\"title\":\"Session-RPE Method for Training Load Monitoring: Validity, Ecological Usefulness, and Influencing Factors - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29163016/\"},{\"title\":\"353 Journal of Strength and Conditioning Research, 2004, 18(2), 353–358\",\"url\":\"http://formacion.ferugby.es/wp-content/uploads/2019/04/Monitoring-Exercise-Intensity-During-Resistance-Training_RPE.pdf\"},{\"title\":\"Session-RPE Method for Training Load Monitoring: Validity, Ecological Usefulness, and Influencing Factors - PMC\",\"url\":\"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5673663/\"},{\"title\":\"Validity of Session Rating Perceived Exertion Method for Quantifying Internal Training Load during High-Intensity Functional Training\",\"url\":\"https://www.mdpi.com/2075-4663/6/3/68\"},{\"title\":\"Session-RPE Method for Training Load Monitoring: Validity, Ecological Usefulness, and Influencing Factors\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5673663/\"}]\n\nI found relevant information about session RPE for resistance training monitoring. Here are the key findings:\n\n## The Day et al. 2004 Study\n\nThe 2004 study investigated the reliability of the session rating of perceived exertion (RPE) scale to quantify exercise intensity during high-intensity, moderate-intensity, and low-intensity resistance training. The study involved nine men and ten women who performed five exercises (back squat, bench press, overhead press, biceps curl, and triceps pushdown) at different intensities: high-intensity (1 set of 4-5 repetitions at 90% 1RM), moderate-intensity (1 set of 10 repetitions at 70% 1RM), and low-intensity (1 set of 15 repetitions at 50% 1RM).\n\n## Validity and Application\n\n36 studies have examined the validity and reliability of the session RPE method using the modified CR-10, confirming the validity and good reliability and internal consistency of session-RPE method in several sports and physical activities with men and women of different age categories among various expertise levels.\n\nSession rating of perceived exertion (RPE) can delineate intensities and may be a useful tool for strength coaches and athletes. The session-RPE method involves multiplying the total duration of a bout or exercise session in minutes by the training intensity.\n\nThis method could be used as a \"standing alone\" method for training load (TL) monitoring purposes though some recommend combining it with other physiological parameters such as heart rate.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"8e902f81-7a96-4e16-ab44-1767f9fa591b","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:54:38.782Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"f74c06d4-8202-4e28-9bb1-204cf8147d8c","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

Powerlifters\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/318166020_Self-Rated_Accuracy_of_Rating_of_Perceived_Exertion-Based_Load_Prescription_in_Powerlifters\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) Rating of Perceived Exertion as a Method of Volume Autoregulation Within a Periodized Program\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/318030802_Rating_of_Perceived_Exertion_as_a_Method_of_Volume_Autoregulation_Within_a_Periodized_Program\"},{\"title\":\"PeerJ Effects of subjective and objective autoregulation methods for intensity and volume on enhancing maximal strength during resistance-training interventions: a systematic review\",\"url\":\"https://peerj.com/articles/10663/\"},{\"title\":\"Rating of Perceived Exertion as a Method of Volume Autoregulation Within a Periodized Program - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29786623/\"}]\n\nI found relevant research on RPE-based load prescription versus percentage-based autoregulation in trained lifters. Here's what the research shows:\n\n## Key Findings from Graham & Cleather and Helms Studies\n\nThe Helms and Graham and Cleather studies found that allowing lifters to choose a load based upon RPE allowed subjects to train at a higher average intensity throughout their respective 8- and 12-week training studies, which led to greater 1RM strength in various compound movements.\n\nGraham and Cleather reported fewer RIR per-set using RIR prescription than percentage-based prescription in a longitudinal study, and this study reported a faster rate of increase in both the back and front squat exercises for the RIR group over 12 weeks in trained men.\n\n## Mechanism of Advantage\n\nBoth Helms et al. and Graham and Cleather observed that lifters in the RIR prescription groups trained, on average, at a higher percentage of 1RM, which possibly accounted for the faster rate of strength gain in Graham and Cleather.\n\nRBT produces a small benefit over percentage-based training (PBT) for strength adaptations in college-aged resistance-trained males and females.\n\nThese randomized studies demonstrate that RPE-based autoregulation offers advantages over fixed percentage-based prescription, primarily because it allows trained lifters to naturally self-regulate to higher intensities across training sessions.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"ded061a5-f2a8-4216-8812-3afbe6109931","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:54:39.135Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"fb368ac5-b3f0-4240-bdba-90a199668fe2","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

Burn The Fat Blog\",\"url\":\"https://www.burnthefatblog.com/a-review-of-micro-gainz-fractional-plates/\"},{\"title\":\"Fractional Plates & PlateMates: Small Weight Increments, Big Gains – Serious Steel Fitness\",\"url\":\"https://www.serioussteel.com/blogs/news/fractional-plates-platemates-small-weight-increments-big-gains\"},{\"title\":\"Calibrated Fractional Plates KG | Micro Loading | IWF Spec | CFF Strength Equipment\",\"url\":\"https://cffstrengthequipment.com/products/cff-calibrated-fractional-weight-plates-kg-micro-loading\"},{\"title\":\"Microloading with Rogue Fractional Olympic Plates | John PhungMicroloading with Rogue Fractional Olympic Plates\",\"url\":\"https://www.johnphung.com/product-reviews/1286/microloading-with-rogue-fractional-olympic-plates/\"}]\n\nI found information about microloading and fractional plates in resistance training progression. Here's what the search results show:\n\n## Overview of Microloading\n\nMicroloading is the method of increasing your lifting load in tiny increments, usually 0.5–2.5 lbs, to ensure continual progressive overload without overwhelming recovery capacity. Instead of large weight jumps, microloading focuses on small weight increments, typically between 0.25 kg and 1 kg, allowing for a more gradual and controlled progression of strength.\n\n## Recommended Increment Sizes\n\nFor upper body lifts, start with 1–2 lbs (0.5–1 kg) per session, and for lower body lifts use 2–2.5 lbs.\n\n## Scientific Support\n\nThe search results reference research on the effectiveness of microloading. For strength-specific goals, similar hypertrophy has been observed across a broad range of loading zones when volume load is matched, so repetitions, sets, and high-quality effort remain useful alternatives, according to Applied Physiology, Nutrition, and Metabolism (2022).\n\n## When Microloading Is Most Effective\n\nMicroloading is most effective for lifts where your working weight is under 60 kg; above that threshold, standard 2.5 kg jumps represent a small enough percentage increase that most lifters can handle them. Fractional plates are most useful where the regular equipment jump is disproportionately large: overhead press, bench press, rows, curls, and some machine or cable exercises.\n\nThe search results indicate that while many sources discuss the practical benefits of microloading, they reference limited formal research specifically comparing 1-2.5 kg increments.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"3766a035-6124-4124-8f15-8c9af8460641","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:54:54.498Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"9fd787e2-7642-4fb8-9450-ea60d1d47d49","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

Here are the results:\n\nWhen training at a specific RM load, it is recommended that 2-10% increase in load be applied when the individual can perform the current workload for one to two repetitions over the desired number.\n\nThis recommendation comes from the American College of Sports Medicine (ACSM) position stand on \"Progression Models in Resistance Training for Healthy Adults.\" The search results show this exact guideline appearing in multiple peer-reviewed sources, including the official PubMed publication and the full position stand document.\n\nAdditionally, the search results provide related recommendations from the same ACSM position stand regarding training frequency and other progression protocols for different training levels (novice, intermediate, and advanced).\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"0d910a25-8cf0-4950-8279-7a9b1218450d","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:55:06.651Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"d05fb2a9-a1c1-4c8f-aa7c-4c71a27a54b4","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

{"parentUuid":"7e8e1503-c920-4e50-a2fe-b02db2346b50","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011Ce6PEAuuJjSYGLc16xeqJ","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_01Kr5dGkDxEMAaF6rRdSNv1n","name":"WebFetch","input":{"url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7575491/","prompt":"Extract: authors, year, journal. Quote key passages verbatim where possible."},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"stop_reason":"tool_use","stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":2,"cache_creation_input_tokens":1716,"cache_read_input_tokens":59241,"output_tokens":244,"output_tokens_details":{"thinking_tokens":0},"server_tool_use":{"web_search_requests":0,"web_fetch_requests":0},"service_tier":"standard","cache_creation":{"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":1716},"inference_geo":"not_available","iterations":[{"input_tokens":2,"output_tokens":244,"cache_read_input_tokens":59241,"cache_creation_input_tokens":1716,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":1716,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"type":"message"}],"speed":"standard"},"diagnostics":null},"requestId":"req_011Ce6PE9wcF36uCkR4eRm9u","attributionAgent":"general-purpose","type":"assistant","uuid":"b196002b-3e41-47d4-9cf9-8cfb51662c75","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:55:28.000Z","effort":"high","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}
{"parentUuid":"b196002b-3e41-47d4-9cf9-8cfb51662c75","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Kr5dGkDxEMAaF6rRdSNv1n","type":"tool_result","content":"# Analysis of Autoregulation in Resistance Training\n\n**Authors & Publication:** Greig et al., 2020, *Sports Medicine*\n\n## (a) APRE Protocol Prescription Rules\n\nThe paper describes Knight's Daily Adjustable Progressive Resistive Exercise (DAPRE) protocol in detail:\n\n**Set Structure (Table 2):**\n- Set 1: 50% of 6RM estimate × 10 reps\n- Set 2: 75% of 6RM estimate × 6 reps\n- Set 3: 100% of 6RM estimate × AMRAP (as many reps as possible)\n- Set 4: Adjusted load × AMRAP\n\n**Load Adjustment Rules (Table 1):** Based on set 3 performance:\n- ≤2 reps: Decrease 5–10 lb (both set 4 and next session)\n- 3–4 reps: Decrease 0–5 lb / Keep same next session\n- 5–6 reps: Keep same / Increase 5–10 lb next session\n- 7–10 reps: Increase 5–10 lb / Increase 5–15 lb next session\n- >10 reps: Increase 10–15 lb / Increase 10–20 lb next session\n\nThe authors note: \"the training generated from the framework was perceived as monotonous.\"\n\n## (b) Within-Session Load Adjustment\n\nThe paper emphasizes two primary methods:\n\n**1. Repetitions in Reserve (RIR):** Practitioners can \"self-select the load which they believe will elicit the corresponding exertion\" based on perceived proximity to failure.\n\n**2. Velocity-Based Training (VBT):** Jovanovic and Flanagan's approach involves \"building individualised load–velocity profiles and subsequently measuring velocity during a multi-set warm-up protocol.\" If observed velocity diverges from target, \"the load–velocity profile could be used to provide an adjusted estimate of the load for subsequent sets.\"\n\n## (c) Day-to-Day Variability and Readiness Rationale\n\nThe paper proposes a reformulated Fitness-Fatigue Model incorporating \"readiness\" as non-training stressor effects:\n\n\"Performance on a given day is equal to the sum of training- and non-training-related components\" including sleep, nutrition, and illness. The authors explain that \"readiness can be conceptualised as a stochastic fluctuation in performance with a mean equal to 0.\"\n\nThey note: \"adjustments to training generally need not be made unless the changes in performance differ from those that are expected as a result of the programme.\"\n\nFor experienced athletes in maintenance phases, \"variations in performance (and therefore any adjustments made to training) will be influenced primarily by readiness.\"\n\n## (d) Limitations and Evidence Gaps\n\n**Key limitation identified:**\n\"Despite substantial developments since the 1940s, the lack of an overarching framework has led to inconsistencies in definitions and terminology.\"\n\n**Specific research gaps:**\n\n- CMJ sensitivity: \"it is unclear whether it provides a sensitive measure of performance change across shorter time intervals.\"\n\n- VBT longitudinal evidence: \"Future research is, therefore, required to evaluate the longitudinal application of these approaches.\"\n\n- RIR volume autoregulation: \"At present, there has been limited research investigating the use of RIR for the autoregulation of training volume.\"\n\n- Psychological outcomes: \"these latter hypotheses have yet to be systematically investigated.\"\n\nThe authors conclude: \"Future research should focus attention on identifying key features of the measurement and adjustment process that can be used to identify and define general autoregulatory principles.\""}]},"uuid":"eb3beff5-fa52-4b4e-8056-9fe3eddaf607","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:55:40.390Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"b196002b-3e41-47d4-9cf9-8cfb51662c75","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}
