{"parentUuid":"4b142a9a-4db2-4723-91be-ebd7298fb44c","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_018DjerjTDiE7PhjCpy2AUFb","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Schoenfeld low load vs high load resistance training hypertrophy meta-analysis equal when sets to failure\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Strength and Hypertrophy Adaptations Between Low- vs. High-Load Resistance Training: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis\",\"url\":\"https://www.ovid.com/jnls/nsca-jscr/pdf/10.1519/jsc.0000000000002200~strength-and-hypertrophy-adaptations-between-low--vs\"},{\"title\":\"[PDF] Strength and Hypertrophy Adaptations Between Low- vs. High-Load Resistance Training: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis | Semantic Scholar\",\"url\":\"https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/Strength-and-Hypertrophy-Adaptations-Between-Low-A-Schoenfeld-Grgic/bf737d9c1090be1a883e2be6873060c6c1c5273e\"},{\"title\":\"Strength and hypertrophy adaptations between low- vs. high-load resistance training: A systematic review and meta-analysis | VU Institutional Repository | Victoria University | Melbourne Australia\",\"url\":\"https://vuir.vu.edu.au/42281/\"},{\"title\":\"Muscular adaptations in low- versus high-load resistance training: A meta-analysis - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25530577/\"},{\"title\":\"Strength and Hypertrophy Adaptations Between Low- vs. High-Load Resistance Training: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28834797/\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) The Effects of Low-Load Vs. High-Load Resistance Training on Muscle Fiber Hypertrophy: A Meta-Analysis\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/342038975_The_Effects_of_Low-Load_Vs_High-Load_Resistance_Training_on_Muscle_Fiber_Hypertrophy_A_Meta-Analysis\"},{\"title\":\"Muscle hypertrophy and strength gains after resistance training with different volume-matched loads: a systematic review and meta-analysis\",\"url\":\"https://cdnsciencepub.com/doi/abs/10.1139/apnm-2021-0515\"},{\"title\":\"Are the Hypertrophic Adaptations to High and Low-Load Resistance Training Muscle Fiber Type Specific?\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5915697/\"},{\"title\":\"Strength and Hypertrophy Adaptations Between Low- vs.... : The Journal of Strength & Conditioning Research\",\"url\":\"https://journals.lww.com/nsca-jscr/fulltext/2017/12000/strength_and_hypertrophy_adaptations_between_low_.31.aspx\"}]\n\nI found search results about Schoenfeld's meta-analyses on low-load versus high-load resistance training and hypertrophy. Here are the key findings:\n\n## Schoenfeld's 2017 Meta-Analysis (21 studies to failure)\n\nThe meta-analysis included studies where all sets in the training protocols were performed to momentary muscular failure, and changes in measures of muscle hypertrophy were similar between low-load and high-load conditions.\n\nMore specifically, gains in 1RM strength were significantly greater in favor of high- vs. low-load training, whereas no significant differences were found for isometric strength between conditions.\n\nThe conclusion from this analysis was that maximal strength benefits are obtained from the use of heavy loads while muscle hypertrophy can be equally achieved across a spectrum of loading ranges.\n\n## Earlier Meta-Analysis (9 studies)\n\nAn earlier Schoenfeld meta-analysis found a trend for hypertrophy outcomes to be greater with high loads compared to low loads, though training with loads ≤50% 1 RM was found to promote substantial increases in muscle strength and hypertrophy in untrained individuals, but a trend was noted for superiority of heavy loading.\n\nThe key point is that when sets are taken to momentary muscular failure, low and high load training produce similar hypertrophy outcomes, though high load training produces greater strength gains.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"4c034d3f-5e7a-439a-b8a2-8e5353e33d8b","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:55:58.597Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"4b142a9a-4db2-4723-91be-ebd7298fb44c","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

JEFIT\",\"url\":\"https://www.jefit.com/blog/progressive-overload-in-2026-the-science-backed-guide-to-continuous-strength-gains\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of Resistance Training Overload Progression Protocols on Strength and Muscle Mass\",\"url\":\"https://ouci.dntb.gov.ua/en/works/4gEjMX19/\"},{\"title\":\"Thieme E-Journals - International Journal of Sports Medicine / Abstract\",\"url\":\"https://www.thieme-connect.com/products/ejournals/abstract/10.1055/a-2256-5857\"},{\"title\":\"Progressive Overload in 2026: The Science-Backed Guide to Continuous Strength Gains | Jefit - #1 Gym / Home workout app\",\"url\":\"https://www.jefit.com/wp/guide/progressive-overload-in-2026-the-science-backed-guide-to-continuous-strength-gains/\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of Resistance Training Overload Progression Protocols ...\",\"url\":\"https://www.thieme-connect.com/products/ejournals/pdf/10.1055/a-2256-5857.pdf\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, I found a highly relevant 2024 study published in the International Journal of Sports Medicine that directly addresses your query about overload progression protocols in resistance training.\n\n## Study Overview\n\nThe study, published in 2024 in the International Journal of Sports Medicine, compared the effects of progressive overload in resistance training by specifically comparing the impact of increasing load (LOADprog) versus an increase in repetitions (REPSprog) on muscle strength and cross-sectional area (CSA).\n\n## Study Design\n\nThe research used a within-subject experimental design in which 39 previously untrained young persons (20 men and 19 women) had their legs randomized to LOADprog and REPSprog.\n\n## Key Findings\n\nBoth protocols increased 1RM values from pre to post, with no difference between them. Similarly, both protocols also increased in CSA values from pre to post, with no difference between them.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nThe findings indicate that the progression of overload through load or repetitions can be used to promote gains in strength and muscle hypertrophy in young men and women in the early stages of training. This suggests that for untrained individuals, both progression strategies are equally effective for building strength and muscle.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"e4a157e3-bce3-445f-9997-ec61ae9afef0","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:56:09.388Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"e281cea8-0122-4217-81bb-5a2f2d7de5d7","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

Mitter - 2023 - European Journal of Sport Science - Wiley Online Library\",\"url\":\"https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1080/17461391.2022.2089915\"},{\"title\":\"Full article: Modelling the relationship between load and repetitions to failure in resistance training: A Bayesian analysis\",\"url\":\"https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/17461391.2022.2089915\"},{\"title\":\"The Influence of Muscular Strength and Local Muscular Endurance on Accuracy of Estimated Repetitions to Failure in Resistance-Trained Males - PMC\",\"url\":\"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8877029/\"},{\"title\":\"Modeling the repetitions‐in‐reserve‐velocity relationship: a valid method for resistance training monitoring and prescription, and fatigue management\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10901726/\"},{\"title\":\"Is Resistance Training to Muscular Failure Necessary? - PMC\",\"url\":\"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4731492/\"}]\n\nI found some relevant research on repetitions to failure and between-session reliability in resistance-trained individuals. Here are the key findings from the search results:\n\n## Between-Session Reliability and Repetitions to Failure\n\nResearch involving 46 resistance-trained people performed a one-repetition maximum test and two repetitions to failure tests 72 hours apart. Individual XRM-velocity relationships established in the first testing session yielded acceptable prediction accuracy with a mean error of 1.96 repetitions in the subsequent testing session.\n\n## Standard Error Measurement\n\nIndividual XRM-velocity relationships demonstrated a residual standard error of 1.01–1.15 repetitions, compared to general relationships with residual standard error of 3.44–3.66 repetitions.\n\n## Trained Individuals and Absolute Load\n\nParticipants performed sets to momentary failure at 90%, 80% and 70% of their 1-RM in a fixed order during a single session when examining the relationship between relative load and repetitions to failure. The research suggests that RIR accuracy is highest among those with sufficient RT experience when training closer to failure with moderate-to-low repetition sets.\n\nThese studies indicate that between-session reliability of repetitions to failure tests at the same absolute load is reasonably good in trained individuals, with individual prediction models showing smaller standard errors than general population models.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"ed4c252b-455f-48b9-ac6d-fa6537cdf20d","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:56:09.940Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"9519d134-2225-41e0-a9ff-8979f23829bb","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

{"parentUuid":"94568ab3-9714-4f8d-99d8-b32a5a1f6e44","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01JsPknXWKCv7UhQqozWUeqc","type":"tool_result","content":"# Study Extraction\n\n**Authors, Year, Journal:**\nJukic et al., 2024, Physiological Reports\n\n**Design:**\n- N = 46 resistance-trained participants (15 females, 31 males)\n- Training status: ≥6 months RT experience, ≥2×/week, back squat ≥1×/week\n- Exercise: Free-weight back squat\n- Sessions: 4 visits over 2 weeks (familiarization, 1RM test, two RTF tests 72h apart)\n- Loads tested: 70%, 80%, 90% of 1RM\n\n**Key Numbers:**\n\n*Residual Standard Error (Individual vs General):*\nIndividual relationships showed substantially lower RSE than general models across loads. Median RSE for individual models ranged approximately 0.5–1.2 repetitions depending on load, while general models displayed considerably higher error.\n\n*Mean Prediction Error (Second Session):*\n\"individual RIR‐velocity relationships established in the first testing session yielded acceptable prediction accuracy of RIR (mean error <2 repetitions) in the subsequent testing session, regardless of the load used\"\n\n*Between-Session Stability:*\n\"Individual, but not general, RIR‐velocity relationships displayed acceptable RSE, regardless of the load or testing session,\" suggesting stable individual profiles across time."}]},"uuid":"8da2a3f4-6828-46f7-94e2-dec7a3e47c7b","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:56:21.065Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"94568ab3-9714-4f8d-99d8-b32a5a1f6e44","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

Refalo - 2025 - European Journal of Sport Science - Wiley Online Library\",\"url\":\"https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/ejsc.12266\"},{\"title\":\"Reproducibility of strength performance and strength-endurance profiles: A test-retest study | PLOS One\",\"url\":\"https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pone.0268074\"}]\n\nI found the Mitter 2023 study! The study by Mitter, B., Zhang, L., Bauer, P., Baca, A. and Tschan, H. (2023) used a Bayesian approach to model the relationship between load and repetitions to failure in resistance training, and was published in the European Journal of Sport Science, volume 23, pages 1203-1213.\n\nHowever, the search results provide limited details about the specific findings regarding between-individual variability, standard deviation of repetitions, and data for the bench press and squat exercises. The search results show that the study identified the relationship between load and the number of repetitions performed to momentary failure in the pin press exercise, but more specific details about the variability metrics and other exercises would require accessing the full article text.\n\nTo get the complete information you're looking for (including the between-individual variability standard deviations for repetitions to failure in bench press and squat), you would need to access the full article at the DOI: 10.1080/17461391.2022.2089915.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"ae0ab3b8-9657-4900-b391-de1f67b18466","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:56:32.201Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"d427b94e-48c9-44be-9dc5-7d35d0036471","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}
{"parentUuid":"d0e4f9e4-3d05-4c07-87f5-6695382428b1","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01BzotJaewQqsEbsrMfeevHS","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"reliability of repetition decrement across sets fatigue index resistance training within-individual reproducible\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Neuromuscular Fatigue During Heavy Resistance Drop Set... : The Journal of Strength & Conditioning Research\",\"url\":\"https://journals.lww.com/nsca-jscr/abstract/9900/neuromuscular_fatigue_during_heavy_resistance_drop.920.aspx\"},{\"title\":\"Relationship Between Fatigue Index and Number of Repetition Maxima with Sub-Maximal Loads in Biceps Curl - PMC\",\"url\":\"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3827769/\"},{\"title\":\"Repeated Resistance Training Reveals the Reproducibility of Muscle Strength and Size Responses Within Individuals\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12659766/\"},{\"title\":\"A Weight-Dependent 1RM Prediction Equation Optimized on 303,494 Near-Failure Sets Across 388 Exercises\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.17495\"},{\"title\":\"Fatigue and Metabolic Responses during Repeated Sets of Bench Press Exercise to Exhaustion at Different Ranges of Motion\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11057609/\"},{\"title\":\"Reproducibility and Applicability of Traditional Strength Training Prescription Recommendations\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9220145/\"},{\"title\":\"Influence of Resistance Training Proximity-to-Failure, Determined by Repetitions-in-Reserve, on Neuromuscular Fatigue in Resistance-Trained Males and Females\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9908800/\"}]\n\nI found several relevant research results on fatigue indices and performance decrement across sets in resistance training. Here are the key findings:\n\n## Fatigue Index and Repetition Decrement Across Sets\n\nDecrement in repetitions performed (RM), time under tension (TUT) and impulse across successive sets can be used as a measure of fatigue. Performance declines across successive sets due to neuromuscular fatigue, and this decline is modulated by rest interval duration.\n\n## Performance Changes Across Sets\n\nResearch demonstrates that significant repetition count declines were observed starting from the second set with 1-minute rest and from the third set with 3- and 5-minute rest. Additionally, the rate of decline in mean barbell velocity doubled from set 1 to set 3.\n\n## Within-Individual Reproducibility\n\nRegarding within-individual reproducibility, it was not possible to complete the same number of repetitions in each set for the same absolute load, and the level of fatigue generated through each set and its relationship with recovery capacity could be different in each individual, showing an important coefficient of variation in each of the sets.\n\nHowever, responses to resistance training are reproducible when resistance training is repeated, indicating that the individuality of the training response has a physiological origin.\n\n## Fatigue Quantification Methods\n\nFatigue may be quantified by measuring the total number of repetitions, the total work done, the percentage drop in performance or the slope of performance decrement.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"975e350a-6361-4fc4-8297-d9728622d51d","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:56:35.125Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"d0e4f9e4-3d05-4c07-87f5-6695382428b1","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

Open (Feb 2023)\",\"url\":\"https://doaj.org/article/f6bb205b05c84a62965ab9a86d839c83\"}]\n\nI found the study you're looking for! Here are the key details:\n\nThe study by Martin C. Refalo and colleagues, published online on February 23, 2024, in the Journal of Sports Sciences, examined similar muscle hypertrophy following eight weeks of resistance training to momentary muscular failure or with repetitions-in-reserve in resistance-trained individuals.\n\nThe study randomized 26 resistance-trained adults to failure training or 1 to 2 reps in reserve for 8 weeks, and found that quadriceps hypertrophy was similar in both groups. The team used a within-subject design where 26 resistance-trained adults trained one leg to momentary muscular failure and the other leg with 1 to 2 reps in reserve.\n\nThe result showed that quadriceps thickness increased similarly in both legs, with the failure leg showing slightly higher reported fatigue and rate of perceived exertion, but no measurable hypertrophy advantage.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"469c1523-4d21-4fe5-85d0-950a0de85601","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:56:46.515Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"449ebcb9-2121-49da-9b85-ef6463334e22","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

{"parentUuid":"e15c846f-ab43-4be6-aed5-87e22a2e3de0","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01YPUHtPzWn7ZLWqsGfYB6W5","type":"tool_result","content":"# Study Analysis: Strength Training Protocol Reproducibility\n\n**Authors & Publication:**\n- Lead author: Juan Ramón Heredia-Elvar\n- Year: 2022\n- Journal: Biology (Basel)\n\n**Study Design:**\n30 male physical education students performed bench press at 70% 1RM for 3 sets to maximum repetitions (MNR) with 2-minute rest intervals between sets.\n\n**Key Findings on Repetition Completion:**\n\n| Set | Mean Reps | SD | CV | Range |\n|-----|-----------|----|----|-------|\n| Set 1 | 12.50 | 2.19 | 17.5% | 8-16 |\n| Set 2 | 6.06 | 1.98 | 32.7% | 2-10 |\n| Set 3 | 4.20 | 1.99 | 47.4% | 2-12 |\n\nThe coefficient of variation increased substantially across successive sets, indicating growing individual variability in performance.\n\n**Critical Conclusion:**\nThe authors state: \"it was not possible to complete the same number of repetitions in each set for the same absolute load.\" They found that \"the fatigue generated in each set could have an individual response depending on the capacity of each subject to recover,\" making traditional fixed-repetition prescriptions (like \"3×10\") difficult to reproduce and control objectively.\n\n**Implication:**\nPrescribing fixed repetitions at constant loads provides inaccurate training stimulus quantification across sets."}]},"uuid":"74534b0d-03b2-418a-80a0-6ef67a2df5a7","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:56:55.860Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"e15c846f-ab43-4be6-aed5-87e22a2e3de0","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}
{"parentUuid":"1b1d521c-e1ae-4824-a523-0f731e8b1c2a","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01GqGt7wJKBr2PeXxpJ8Peei","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"readiness questionnaire wellness rating predict resistance training performance autoregulation subjective session rating validity strength\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"PeerJ Effects of subjective and objective autoregulation methods for intensity and volume on enhancing maximal strength during resistance-training interventions: a systematic review\",\"url\":\"https://peerj.com/articles/10663/\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of subjective and objective autoregulation methods for intensity and\",\"url\":\"https://pdfs.semanticscholar.org/78d0/50d9d42eefd94f619b587a58f3e1610e0f0b.pdf\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) Autoregulation in Resistance Training: A Comparison of Subjective Versus Objective Methods\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/339281591_Autoregulation_in_Resistance_Training_A_Comparison_of_Subjective_Versus_Objective_Methods\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of subjective and objective autoregulation methods for intensity and volume on enhancing maximal strength during resistance-training interventions: a systematic review\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7810043/\"},{\"title\":\"The Effects of Percentage-Based, Rating of Perceived Exertion, Repetitions in Reserve, and Velocity-Based Training on Performance and Fatigue Responses\",\"url\":\"https://www.ovid.com/jnls/nsca-jscr/fulltext/10.1519/jsc.0000000000005026~the-effects-of-percentage-based-rating-of-perceived-exertion\"},{\"title\":\"Autoregulation in strength training: avoid overtraining and undertraining\",\"url\":\"https://gymaware.com/autoregulation-in-strength-training/\"},{\"title\":\"Readiness In Athlete Monitoring: Is Your Score Really Valid?\",\"url\":\"https://www.omni-athlete.com/post/readiness-in-athlete-monitoring-is-your-score-really-valid\"}]\n\nBased on the web search results, here's what I found on this topic:\n\n## Autoregulation in Resistance Training\n\nAutoregulation is a resistance training prescription approach to adjust training variables based on the individuals' daily fluctuations in performance, which are a result of training-induced fitness and fatigue, together with readiness from daily non-training stressors.\n\n## Readiness and Wellness Measures\n\nBrief wellness questionnaires covering perceived fatigue, sleep quality, muscle soreness, stress, and mood, together with session ratings of perceived exertion, capture the athlete's integrated experience of load and recovery. This could be done by rating readiness on a 0–10 subjective scale, and thereby choose the training stimulus to be either hypertrophy, strength, or power specific for the training session.\n\n## Validity and Performance Prediction\n\nSubjective and objective measures generally did not correlate with one another, and subjective measures reflected acute and chronic training loads with greater sensitivity and consistency than the objective measures examined. Additionally, RPE and RIR, which both use subjective information, have demonstrated significantly greater strength adaptations in comparison with %1RM methods for 8–12 weeks training cycles.\n\nThe research suggests that subjective readiness questionnaires and wellness ratings can be valuable tools for predicting and optimizing resistance training performance when used as part of an autoregulation strategy.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"ecedcf47-e8bb-4d67-b878-a5c1dd8ddae9","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:56:57.157Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"1b1d521c-e1ae-4824-a523-0f731e8b1c2a","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

Leeds Beckett Repository\",\"url\":\"https://eprints.leedsbeckett.ac.uk/id/eprint/11806/\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) The Effects of Percentage-Based, Rating of Perceived Exertion, Repetitions in Reserve, and Velocity-Based Training on Performance and Fatigue Responses\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/387872085_The_Effects_of_Percentage-Based_Rating_of_Perceived_Exertion_Repetitions_in_Reserve_and_Velocity-Based_Training_on_Performance_and_Fatigue_Responses\"},{\"title\":\"The Effects of Percentage-Based, Rating of Perceived Exertion, ...\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/profile/Jonathon-Weakley/publication/387872085_The_Effects_of_Percentage-Based_Rating_of_Perceived_Exertion_Repetitions_in_Reserve_and_Velocity-Based_Training_on_Performance_and_Fatigue_Responses/links/678070b918ad70589ea7972f/The-Effects-of-Percentage-Based-Rating-of-Perceived-Exertion-Repetitions-in-Reserve-and-Velocity-Based-Training-on-Performance-and-Fatigue-Responses.pdf\"},{\"title\":\"The Effects of Percentage-Based, Rating of Perceived Exertion, Repetitions in Reserve, and Velocity-Based Training on Performance and Fatigue Responses\",\"url\":\"https://www.ovid.com/jnls/nsca-jscr/fulltext/10.1519/jsc.0000000000005026~the-effects-of-percentage-based-rating-of-perceived-exertion\"},{\"title\":\"The Effects of Percentage-Based, Rating of Perceived... : The Journal of Strength & Conditioning Research\",\"url\":\"https://journals.lww.com/nsca-jscr/fulltext/2025/04000/the_effects_of_percentage_based,_rating_of.13.aspx\"}]\n\n## Search Results\n\nI found the study you're looking for. Here's the information:\n\n**Citation and Publication Details:**\nThe study \"The Effects of Percentage-Based, Rating of Perceived Exertion, Repetitions in Reserve, and Velocity-Based Training on Performance and Fatigue Responses\" was authored by Cowley, N, Nicholson, V, Timmins, R, Munteanu, G, Wood, T, García-Ramos, A, Owen, C, and Weakley, J, and was published in the Journal of Strength and Conditioning Research, volume 39(4), pages e516-e529 in 2025.\n\n**Study Overview:**\nThe study assessed the effects of percentage-based training (%1RM), rating of perceived exertion (RPE), repetitions in reserve (RIR), and velocity-based training (VBT) on acute kinematic outputs, perceptions of effort, and changes in neuromuscular function during resistance training, as well as neuromuscular fatigue and perceptions of soreness 24 hours after exercise.\n\n**Methodology:**\nIn a randomized crossover design, 15 subjects completed a %1RM, RPE, RIR, and VBT training condition involving a fatiguing protocol followed by 5 sets of the free-weight back squat and bench press at 70% of 1 repetition maximum.\n\n**Key Findings:**\nPercentage-based training and RPE allowed the smallest volume loads, with %1RM prescription causing sets to be regularly taken to failure, while RIR and VBT allowed greater maintenance of training volume.\n\nYou can access the full article through the Journal of Strength and Conditioning Research or the Leeds Beckett Repository.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"112ff809-be7f-4b85-b5a9-10018cc912b5","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:57:12.088Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"f6e1e435-1ed1-41c8-b373-dcb4c2debf77","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}
{"parentUuid":"b0a5884e-dbdc-4b40-9b19-99fb6563f5a6","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01D7GpCxznRrVmYcxTV8Bcxn","type":"tool_result","content":"# Systematic Review: Autoregulation vs. Fixed Loading for Maximal Strength\n\n**Journal:** PeerJ (2021)\n**Authors:** Larsen, Kristiansen, van den Tillaar\n\n---\n\n## Included Studies Comparing Autoregulated vs. Fixed/Percentage-Based Loading\n\n### Subjective Autoregulation Methods\n\n**1. RPE/RIR Studies**\n\n| Study | N | Training Status | Duration | Result |\n|-------|---|-----------------|----------|--------|\n| Helms et al. (2018a) | 21 | Resistance-trained males | 8 weeks × 3/week | RPE: ES 0.64 (squat), 0.55 (bench). Similar to control; not significant |\n| Graham & Cleather (2019) | 31 | Resistance-trained males | 12 weeks × 2/week | RIR: ES 0.53 (front squat), 0.51 (back squat). RIR superior to fixed loading |\n| Shattock & Tee (2020) | 20 | Rugby players | 12 weeks | RPE group showed smaller gains than velocity-based comparison |\n\n**2. Autoregulatory Progressive Resistance Exercise (APRE)**\n\n| Study | N | Training Status | Duration | Result |\n|-------|---|-----------------|----------|--------|\n| Mann et al. (2010) | 23 | College football players | 6 weeks × 3/week | APRE: ES 0.66 (squat), 0.83 (bench). Superior to linear periodization |\n\n### Objective Autoregulation Methods\n\n**3. Velocity Targets**\n\n| Study | N | Training Status | Duration | Result |\n|-------|---|-----------------|----------|--------|\n| Dorrell, Smith & Gee (2019) | 16 | Resistance-trained males | 6 weeks × 2/week | VBRT: ES 0.53 (squat), 0.54 (bench). ~50% greater gains than percent-based |\n| Orange et al. (2019) | 27 | Junior rugby players | 7 weeks × 2/week | VBRT: ES 0.46 (squat). Similar to control |\n| Shattock & Tee (2020) | 10 | Rugby players | 12 weeks | VBRT superior to RPE at 12 weeks |\n\n**4. Velocity Loss Thresholds**\n\n| Study | N | Training Status | Duration | Result |\n|-------|---|-----------------|----------|--------|\n| Pareja-Blanco et al. (2017a) | 22 | Resistance-trained males | 8 weeks × 2/week | VL20%: ES 1.53; VL40%: ES 0.79 |\n| Pareja-Blanco et al. (2017b) | 16 | Soccer players | 6 weeks × 3/week | VL15%: ES 0.54; VL30%: ES 0.25 |\n| Galiano et al. (2020) | 30 | Resistance-trained males | 7 weeks × 2/week | VL5%: ES 0.74; VL20%: ES 0.81 |\n| Pareja-Blanco et al. (2020) | 55 | Resistance-trained males | 8 weeks × 2/week | VL0%: ES 0.66; VL10%: ES 1.1; VL20%: ES 0.61; VL40%: ES 0.62 |\n| Rodríguez-Rosell et al. (2020) | 25 | Resistance-trained males | 8 weeks × 2/week | VL10%: ES 0.70; VL30%: ES 0.93 |\n| Sánchez-Moreno et al. (2020) | 29 | Resistance-trained males | Pull-ups | VL25%: ES 0.61; VL50%: ES 0.04 |\n\n---\n\n## Overall Conclusion\n\nThe authors state: *\"Overall, our findings suggest that using both subjective autoregulation methods for intensity, such as repetitions in reserve rating of perceived exertion and flexible daily undulation periodisation, together with objective autoregulation methods for autoregulation intensity and volume, such as velocity targets and velocity loss, could be effective methods for enhancing maximal strength.\"*\n\nThey speculate this effectiveness occurs because *\"the implementation of autoregulation into a periodised plan may take into account the athletes' daily fluctuations, such as fluctuations in fitness, fatigue, and readiness to train.\"*\n\n### Key Caveats About Study Quality\n\n- **Small sample sizes** limit generalizability\n- **Limited female participant representation** across studies\n- **Methodological limitations** in Mann et al. (2010): no randomization, unmatched baseline strength, unclear baseline testing procedures\n- **Velocity loss studies** predominantly used Smith machine (limited ecological validity); four of six used estimated rather than actual 1-RM\n- **RPE accuracy concerns:** Less reliable in untrained populations and high-repetition sets\n- **Velocity measurement device inaccuracies** documented in some systems\n- **Training intervention duration variation** (6–12 weeks) affects neural vs. muscular adaptation attribution"}]},"uuid":"6bf1af92-0e66-43f1-a09d-b58b740a908f","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:57:20.823Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"b0a5884e-dbdc-4b40-9b19-99fb6563f5a6","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

{"parentUuid":"eedbc748-f1bc-4f3a-9d22-ebd3af91453d","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011Ce6PPUYJMDFcYbfDUVycQ","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_01PCqd2KN61CEuh85SE4b649","name":"WebSearch","input":{"query":"Robinson Pelland Zourdos proximity to failure meta-regression \"0 RIR\" hypertrophy effect size slope per repetition in reserve results"},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":2,"cache_creation_input_tokens":2812,"cache_read_input_tokens":77965,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":2812,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":17,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"diagnostics":null},"requestId":"req_011Ce6PPTfDfWHJ61pHz8VBW","attributionAgent":"general-purpose","type":"assistant","uuid":"44bcb214-1d1a-4ac8-9bd7-60a509d215c2","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:57:34.820Z","effort":"high","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}
{"parentUuid":"eedbc748-f1bc-4f3a-9d22-ebd3af91453d","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Vzs2xkr6RyFWfWJxW8ja4Q","type":"tool_result","content":"# Meta-Analysis Summary: Proximity to Failure and Resistance Training Outcomes\n\n## Abstract\nThe study examined the dose-response relationship between estimated repetitions in reserve (RIR) and both strength and muscle hypertrophy outcomes through meta-regressions.\n\n## Key Findings\n\n**Study Scope:**\nThe abstract does not explicitly state the total number of studies or effects analyzed. The document references \"a series of exploratory multi-level meta-regressions\" but specific numerical counts are not provided in the abstract section.\n\n**RIR Range:**\nThe exact range of estimated RIR examined is not specified in the available abstract content.\n\n**Results - Strength Outcomes:**\n\"In all of the best-fit models for strength, the confidence intervals of the marginal slopes for estimated RIR contained a null point estimate, indicating a negligible relationship\" with strength gains.\n\n**Results - Hypertrophy Outcomes:**\n\"In all of the best-fit models for muscle hypertrophy, the marginal slopes for estimated RIR were negative and their confidence intervals did not contain a null point estimate,\" suggesting improvements as sets approached failure.\n\n**Practical Magnitude:**\n\"Strength gains were similar across a wide range of RIR, while muscle hypertrophy improves as sets are terminated closer to failure.\"\n\n## Conclusion\nThe authors note that \"the exact relationship between RIR and muscle hypertrophy and strength remains unclear\" due to RIR estimation procedures, recommending caution in interpretation."}]},"uuid":"68b48c0a-dc40-4d90-a202-a924fbe09a6c","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:57:39.480Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"eedbc748-f1bc-4f3a-9d22-ebd3af91453d","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pubmed/22130393\"},{\"title\":\"Effect of load reductions on performance of a strength training session with different exercise orders - ScienceDirect\",\"url\":\"https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0765159724000509\"}]\n\nI found research on this topic. Here are the key findings related to your query:\n\n## Willardson Research on Load Reductions and Squat Performance\n\nDuring a squat protocol with 1-minute rest intervals, men needed a 15% reduction in load for each set in succession to maintain repetition number, whereas women needed only a 10% reduction.\n\nMore specifically, the back squat and leg curl required 15% load reductions per set to maintain repetition performance. This finding comes from research by Willardson and colleagues who examined the effects of different load reduction strategies (0%, 5%, 10%, and 15%) on maintaining 10 repetitions across multiple sets.\n\nSubjects were able to maintain a 10-RM range for all three sets with the use of a 15% drop in resistance after the first and second set in the back squat and leg curl.\n\nThe research shows that for the leg extension, there were no significant differences in the repetitions accomplished between conditions, meaning this exercise did not require load reductions to maintain performance across sets.\n\nThis research has been influential in the strength and conditioning field, establishing practical guidelines for load management during resistance training sessions when the goal is to maintain a specific repetition target across consecutive sets.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"29cb9428-8d00-4ad6-85ef-8696acb241d0","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:57:56.761Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"4fafc480-cbe2-4c37-903e-89fc260571d2","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

Räntilä - 2025 - European Journal of Sport Science - Wiley Online Library\",\"url\":\"https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/ejsc.70095\"},{\"title\":\"Repeated Resistance Training Reveals the Reproducibility of Muscle Strength and Size Responses Within Individuals\",\"url\":\"https://www.ovid.com/journals/ejoss/fulltext/10.1002/ejsc.70095~repeated-resistance-training-reveals-the-reproducibility-of\"},{\"title\":\"Repeated Resistance Training Reveals the Reproducibility ...\",\"url\":\"https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/pdfdirect/10.1002/ejsc.70095\"},{\"title\":\"Repeated Resistance Training Reveals the Reproducibility of Muscle Strength and Size Responses Within Individuals - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41307987/\"},{\"title\":\"Repeated Resistance Training Reveals the Reproducibility of Muscle Strength and Size Responses Within Individuals\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12659766/\"},{\"title\":\"Limited reproducibility of individual physiological adaptations to repeated endurance exercise training | Journal of Applied Physiology | American Physiological Society\",\"url\":\"https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/japplphysiol.00154.2026\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what I found about individual variability and reproducibility in resistance training responses:\n\n## Key Findings on Reproducibility\n\nA 2025 study demonstrated reproducibility of responses within participants through correlations between the first and second resistance training periods in vastus lateralis cross-sectional area (r = 0.697), biceps brachii cross-sectional area (r = 0.761), and leg press one-repetition maximum (r = 0.671).\n\n## Study Design\n\nThe study investigated whether responses of upper and lower limb muscle strength and size to resistance training are reproducible across two resistance training periods, with the resistance training group completing two identical 10-week resistance training periods separated by 10 weeks of detraining.\n\n## Main Conclusion\n\nResponses to resistance training are reproducible when resistance training is repeated, indicating that the individuality of the training response has a physiological origin. Nonresponders were identified, but none were detected in both resistance training periods for more than one variable.\n\n## Additional Insights\n\nFirst resistance training gains correlated negatively with subsequent detraining in biceps brachii cross-sectional area and leg press strength, suggesting that greater resistance training gains may also diminish faster.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"0bfb0cd6-230b-4304-a07f-4937d88f4d79","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:58:07.949Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"03b69077-a926-4c20-a47e-df5d05ab1acc","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

Dumbbell Micro Weights – Adding Weight in Tiny Increments\",\"url\":\"https://www.tworepcave.com/2483/4-solutions-for-microloading-adding-weight-in-tiny-increments/\"},{\"title\":\"Micro Gainz l Get More From Your Dumbbells\",\"url\":\"https://microgainz.com/\"},{\"title\":\"Small Hand Weight Set\",\"url\":\"https://www.walmart.com/c/kp/small-hand-weight-set\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what I found regarding load increments for gym equipment and novice lifters:\n\n## Standard Load Increments\n\n**Weight Stack Machines:**\nCommercial selectorized machines commonly use 10 lb, 15 lb, or 20 lb stack jumps, while some machines include a smaller add on weight. However, some machines may have smaller increments, such as 2.5 pounds, to allow for more precise adjustments.\n\n**Dumbbells:**\nSome dumbbells come in 2.5lb increments, though this requires purchasing a whole extra set of in-between sized dumbbells. Non-adjustable dumbbells in 5lb size increments force you to jump 10 lbs for every exercise involving two hands, resulting in big 5lb jumps when you're using two dumbbells.\n\n## The Problem for Novice Lifters\n\nA lifter using 50 lb with a 10 lb jump faces a much bigger relative increase than a lifter using 200 lb with the same 10 lb jump. This relative percentage problem is particularly important for beginners. Smaller weight jumps, such as 5-pound increments, are ideal for beginners, those recovering from injury, or anyone focused on gradual strength progression.\n\n## Solutions\n\nMost weight machines increase in large jumps — often 10 to 15 pounds at a time — which can make steady progression difficult, especially on isolation exercises or when you're close to your limit. Specialized GymPins solve this problem by allowing you to add smaller, more precise weight increases to your machine's weight stack.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"53e5d667-17a6-4ace-8204-c039bc418625","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:58:24.440Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"9e1c5907-eca6-4ae4-a374-7fcc4b32530b","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

{"parentUuid":"911139a0-9849-4c24-abe2-b6dba6ad236d","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_0169gDPbvMpLVSzkeMuxrss9","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Nunes 2021 \"exercise order\" systematic review meta-analysis strength gains hypertrophy first in sequence greater gains conclusion\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"What influence does resistance exercise order have on muscular strength gains and muscle hypertrophy? A systematic review and meta‐analysis - Nunes - 2021 - European Journal of Sport Science - Wiley Online Library\",\"url\":\"https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1080/17461391.2020.1733672\"},{\"title\":\"What influence does resistance exercise order have on muscular strength gains and muscle hypertrophy? A systematic review and meta-analysis - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32077380/\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) What influence does resistance exercise order have on muscular strength gains and muscle hypertrophy? A systematic review and meta-analysis\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/339365541_What_influence_does_resistance_exercise_order_have_on_muscular_strength_gains_and_muscle_hypertrophy_A_systematic_review_and_meta-analysis\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) What influence does resistance exercise order have on muscular strength gains and muscle hypertrophy? A systematic review and meta-analysis\",\"url\":\"https://www.academia.edu/51615911/What_influence_does_resistance_exercise_order_have_on_muscular_strength_gains_and_muscle_hypertrophy_A_systematic_review_and_meta_analysis\"},{\"title\":\"Resistance Training Load Effects on Muscle Hypertrophy and Strength Gain: Systematic Review and Network Meta-analysis - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33433148/\"},{\"title\":\"Your First Exercise Gets Stronger, Not Bigger (11 Studies) — FitChef\",\"url\":\"https://fitchef.com/studies/exercise-order-strength-hypertrophy/\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of Resistance Training Performed with Different Loads in Untrained and Trained Male Adult Individuals on Maximal Strength and Muscle Hypertrophy: A Systematic Review\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8582674/\"}]\n\nI found the Nunes 2021 systematic review and meta-analysis on exercise order. Here are the key findings regarding your query:\n\n## Conclusion on Strength Gains and Hypertrophy\n\nNo significant effect of exercise order was observed for hypertrophy combining site-specific and indirect measures (ES = 0.03; p = 0.862).\n\nHowever, the results for strength gains were different. When all strength tests, that is, both in multi-joint and single-joint exercises were considered, there was no difference between the exercise orders (ES = -0.11; p = 0.306). \n\nMore specifically, exercise order significantly affects strength gains through a specificity principle: whichever exercise is performed first in a training session gets the largest strength boost over weeks of training.\n\n## Summary\n\nThe Nunes 2021 meta-analysis concluded that performing an exercise first in sequence did **not** produce greater hypertrophy gains overall. However, the exercise performed first did receive a strength advantage. Nunes and colleagues pooled data from 11 resistance training studies (268 participants) and found that exercise order has no effect on muscle hypertrophy.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"02a5f880-e5c3-4114-b568-86d1630ef107","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:58:46.530Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"911139a0-9849-4c24-abe2-b6dba6ad236d","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}
{"parentUuid":"50550cf9-7012-497c-aa37-967627bbefcb","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"feba0f58-ad37-4446-821a-203692276616","agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01KX2Q3N9jKRspi4BohJeUvK","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Refalo proximity to failure perceptual responses session RPE motivation enjoyment resistance trained 2025 findings\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"The Effect of Proximity‐To‐Failure on Perceptual Responses to Resistance Training - Refalo - 2025 - European Journal of Sport Science - Wiley Online Library\",\"url\":\"https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/ejsc.12266\"},{\"title\":\"The Effect of Proximity-To-Failure on Perceptual Responses to Resistance Training - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39960821/\"},{\"title\":\"The Effect of Proximity‐To‐Failure on Perceptual Responses to Resistance Training\",\"url\":\"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11832030/\"},{\"title\":\"Quantitation of Resistance Training Using the Session Rating of Perceived Exertion Method | Request PDF\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/8153742_Quantitation_of_Resistance_Training_Using_the_Session_Rating_of_Perceived_Exertion_Method\"},{\"title\":\"Strength & Conditioning Coach Course Series: Should We Train to Failure? | TRAINFITNESS\",\"url\":\"https://train.fitness/personal-trainer-blogs/should-we-train-to-failure\"},{\"title\":\"Influence of Resistance Training Proximity-to-Failure, Determined by Repetitions-in-Reserve, on Neuromuscular Fatigue in Resistance-Trained Males and Females | Sports Medicine - Open | Springer Nature Link\",\"url\":\"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40798-023-00554-y\"}]\n\nI found the research you're looking for. Here's a summary of the 2025 Refalo study on proximity to failure and perceptual responses:\n\n## Study Overview\n\nResistance-trained males (n=12) and females (n=6) completed an 8-week intervention involving two resistance training sessions per week, with lower limbs randomized to perform leg press and leg extension exercises either to momentary muscular failure (FAIL) or with 2-RIR and 1-RIR respectively.\n\n## Measurements\n\nIn weeks one, four, and eight, post-set ratings of perceived discomfort (RPD), and post-session ratings of perceived exertion (RPE) and general feelings via feeling scale (FS) were measured.\n\n## Key Findings\n\nIndividuals' perceptions of discomfort and exertion, along with their affective valence, should be considered when prescribing proximity-to-failure during resistance training. Additionally, individuals who experience displeasure from reaching momentary muscular failure may benefit from terminating sets with 1- to 2-RIR to minimize negative feelings, support comparable muscle hypertrophy and strength outcomes, and possibly enhance long-term adherence.\n\nThe study was published in the European Journal of Sport Science in March 2025 and used Bayesian mixed-effect models for data analysis.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"363f28e7-5d6f-4eda-a9c9-e00bca677236","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:58:48.041Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"50550cf9-7012-497c-aa37-967627bbefcb","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

ScienceDirect\",\"url\":\"https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S1728869X25000590\"},{\"title\":\"Effects of Progressive and Velocity-Based Autoregulatory Resistance Training on Lower-Limb Movement Ability in Taekwondo Athletes - Zijing Huang, Jing Dai, Lunxin Chen, Liang Yang, Min Gong, Duanying Li, Jian Sun, 2025\",\"url\":\"https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/19417381241262024\"},{\"title\":\"The effect of autoregulatory progressive resistance exercise vs. linear periodization on strength improvement in college athletes - PubMed\",\"url\":\"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20543732/\"}]\n\nI found the study! Here are the key results:\n\n## Study Overview\n\nThe study compared eight weeks of auto-regulatory progressive resistance exercise (APRE) to linear programming resistance exercise (LPRE) on changes in muscular strength and endurance, anaerobic power, and body composition in recreationally active males, with twenty-four recreationally active males (age: 24 ± 3 y; body mass: 78.3 ± 10.3 kg) randomly assigned to one of two groups: APRE (n = 12) and LPRE (n = 12).\n\n## Methodology\n\nBoth groups performed supervised training 3x/week for eight weeks, with upper and lower body muscular strength and endurance, anaerobic power, and body composition assessed at baseline, week 4, and 48 h after the final training session.\n\n## Key Results\n\nAfter training, there was a significant increase in absolute leg press (APRE: ES = 2.23; LPRE: ES = 1.35) and chest press strength (APRE: ES = 2.19; LPRE: ES = 0.98), upper (APRE: ES = 2.50; LPRE: ES = 1.074), and lower body peak power. Following APRE, absolute leg press strength was significantly greater compared to LPRE (p= 0.04).\n\nThe study was published in the **European Journal of Sport Science** in 2022 and authored by Ghobadi H, Hosseini SRA, Rashidlamir A, and Forbes SC.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"798db557-e951-4b23-b880-4dc364a0f513","timestamp":"2026-08-16T11:59:01.318Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"3ffbb2e9-a84e-4997-8224-3476cbb6e9fd","userType":"external","entrypoint":"remote_mobile","cwd":"/home/user/ADPhysique","sessionId":"541d0237-403a-50d5-b95e-ecb081e04ba8","version":"2.1.233","gitBranch":"claude/logger-visual-architecture","slug":"splendid-honking-volcano"}

{"parentUuid":"7af7fb04-1da1-474d-a213-26a73e03f30c","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"ab7f9f23d9e951744","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011Ce6PWAkAtiQexPV2Yts4R","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"text","text":"## LIVE SET PRESCRIPTION — EVIDENCE BASE\n\nClassification key: **[S]** SUPPORTED (direct experimental/meta-analytic) · **[R]** REASONABLE COACHING INFERENCE (consistent with evidence, standard practice, not directly tested) · **[X]** UNSUPPORTED / DO NOT CLAIM.\n\n---\n\n### 1. Progressive overload modalities: rep vs load vs double vs mixed\n\n- Load progression and rep progression produced **equivalent** outcomes over 8 weeks in resistance-trained adults: rectus femoris thickness modestly favoured REPS (2.8 mm adjusted effect, CI90% −0.5 to 5.8 mm), 1RM slightly favoured LOAD (2.0 kg, CI −2.4 to 7.8); authors concluded both are \"viable strategies\" (Plotkin et al., 2022, *PeerJ*). **[S]**\n- Replicated in untrained lifters with a within-subject (contralateral leg) design: LOADprog vs REPSprog both increased 1RM and CSA with **no between-protocol difference** (Kassiano/Ribeiro-group study, 2024, *Int J Sports Med*, doi 10.1055/a-2256-5857). **[S]**\n- Because load and reps are near-interchangeable at the group level, the *modality* of overload matters much less than that overload occurs at all — i.e. the resolver may choose whichever axis is practical for the equipment without an evidence penalty. **[R]**\n- Hypertrophy is similar across a wide load spectrum when sets are taken near failure; strength favours heavier loads (Schoenfeld et al., 2017, *J Strength Cond Res*, meta-analysis of 21 studies). This licenses rep-based progression for hypertrophy but means a **strength**-oriented user still needs load to climb eventually. **[S]**\n- **Double progression specifically (fill the range, then add load) has never been tested as a named intervention against alternatives.** It is a scheduling convention that happens to be consistent with the load/reps equivalence data. Do not describe it as \"evidence-based\". **[X]** for the specific claim; **[R]** as a defensible default.\n- The closest institutional endorsement is ACSM's rule: increase load 2–10% \"when the individual can perform the current workload for one to two repetitions over the desired number\" (ACSM Position Stand, 2009, *Med Sci Sports Exerc*) — a *consensus recommendation*, not a trial result. **[R]**\n\n### 2. Must EVERY set hit the top of the range? Increment sizes\n\n- **No study has compared all-sets vs first-set vs majority-of-sets criteria for triggering a load increase.** Any rule Volyume picks here is a design choice, not a finding. **[X]** for claiming a criterion is evidence-based.\n- Strong empirical reason *against* an all-sets criterion: at a fixed load with fixed rest, later sets systematically produce fewer reps, so \"all sets at the top of the range\" is arithmetically near-unreachable for most users (see §3). Requiring it would stall progression indefinitely (Heredia-Elvar et al., 2022, *Biology (Basel)*; Miranda et al., 2009, *J Sports Sci Med*). **[S]** for the underlying fatigue fact; **[R]** for the design implication.\n- ACSM's own trigger is explicitly **not** all-sets: it is \"the current workload for one to two repetitions over the desired number\", conventionally read on the first/best working set, sustained over **two consecutive sessions** (ACSM, 2009). The two-session confirmation is the only widely codified anti-noise mechanism in mainstream guidance. **[R]**\n- Increment size: ACSM 2–10% of current load is the only cited numeric range in a major position stand (ACSM, 2009). **[R]** (consensus, not trial-derived).\n- Knight's DAPRE/APRE tables prescribe **absolute** increments (5–10 lb, up to 10–20 lb for large overshoots), not percentages, and these were used in the trials that showed APRE benefit (tables reproduced in Greig et al., 2020, *Sports Medicine*). So absolute increments have at least indirect trial provenance. **[S]** that trials used absolute increments; **[X]** that absolute beats percentage.\n- No study has compared increment *sizes* (e.g. 2.5% vs 5% vs 10%) head-to-head for hypertrophy or strength. **[X]**\n\n### 3. Within-session fatigue: rep drop-off at fixed load and fixed rest\n\n- Best single dataset for magnitude — bench press at 70% 1RM, 3 sets to maximum reps, **2 min rest**: set 1 = 12.50 ± 2.19, set 2 = 6.06 ± 1.98, set 3 = 4.20 ± 1.99 reps (Heredia-Elvar et al., 2022, *Biology (Basel)*, n=30). Drop-off set1→set2 ≈ 52%; set1→set3 ≈ 66%. Note: **all sets taken to failure**, which maximises decrement. **[S]**\n- Same study: within-set CV rose across sets — 17.5% (set 1), 32.7% (set 2), 47.4% (set 3). **Variability grows faster than the mean falls**, so later-set reps are a much noisier signal than first-set reps. **[S]** — this is the single most important number for the resolver.\n- Rest interval is the dominant moderator. At 8RM, 3 sets: bench press 8.40→6.42→4.17 with 1 min rest (−50.4%) vs 8.30→7.33→5.92 with 3 min rest (−28.7%); triceps pushdown −57.9% (1 min) vs −19.1% (3 min) (Miranda et al., 2009, *J Sports Sci Med*). **[S]**\n- Four sets at 8RM, total reps: back squat 22.47 (1 min) / 25.53 (2 min) / 28.80 (5 min); bench press 17.13 / 21.60 / 25.73 (Willardson & Burkett, 2005–2006, *J Strength Cond Res*). **[S]**\n- Near-maximal loads with long rest degrade later: at an absolute 3RM across 5 sets, reductions began at set 2 with 1 min rest but not until sets 3–4 with 2, 3 or 5 min rest (Scudese et al., 2015, *J Strength Cond Res*). **[S]**\n- Exercise size moderates: to hold 10 reps across 3 sets with 1 min rest, back squat and leg curl needed **~15% load reduction per set**, while leg extension needed none (Willardson et al., 2011/2012, *J Strength Cond Res*); a related report found men needed ~15% and women ~10% in the squat. **[S]** — implies compound lifts and female users need different decrement assumptions.\n- Practical reading for a resolver with 2–3 min rest and sets taken *near* but not to failure: expect roughly **0–1 rep loss per set on isolation/machine work and 1–2 reps per set on large compounds**, with wide individual spread. This interpolation between the failure-based studies above and the non-failure literature is **[R]**, not measured.\n\n### 4. Autoregulating later sets from earlier-set performance TODAY\n\n- APRE/DAPRE is the canonical protocol and it does exactly this: set 3 is an AMRAP at the estimated 6RM, and **set 4's load is adjusted from set 3's rep count** (≤2 reps → −5–10 lb; 3–4 → −0–5 lb; 5–6 → same; 7–10 → +5–10 lb; >10 → +10–15 lb), with a second table setting the next session's load (Knight's DAPRE tables, reproduced in Greig et al., 2020, *Sports Medicine*). **[S]** that this is the prescribed rule.\n- APRE beat linear periodization over 6 weeks in D1 footballers: bench 1RM +93.4 ± 103 N vs −0.40 ± 49.6 N (p=0.02); estimated squat 1RM +192.7 ± 199 N vs +37.2 ± 155 N (p=0.05); reps at 225 lb +3.17 vs −0.09 (p=0.02) (Mann et al., 2010, *J Strength Cond Res*). **[S]** — but the review flags no randomisation, unmatched baselines, unclear testing (Larsen et al., 2021, *PeerJ*).\n- Replicated directionally in recreationally active males over 8 weeks: APRE > linear for absolute leg press strength (p=0.04); chest press ES 2.19 (APRE) vs 0.98 (LPRE) (Ghobadi et al., 2022, *Eur J Sport Sci*, n=24). **[S]**\n- RIR/RPE-based load selection outperformed fixed percentage loading over 12 weeks (front squat ES 0.53, back squat ES 0.51; Graham & Cleather, 2021, *J Strength Cond Res*, n=31), with a smaller/non-significant advantage over 8 weeks in Helms et al., 2018 (squat ES 0.64, bench ES 0.55). The proposed mechanism is that autoregulated groups **trained at a higher average %1RM**, not that the algorithm was smarter (Larsen, Kristiansen & van den Tillaar, 2021, *PeerJ* systematic review). **[S]**\n- Acute mechanism evidence: after a fatiguing protocol, %1RM prescription \"caused sets to be regularly taken to failure\" whereas RIR and VBT prescriptions \"allowed greater maintenance of training volume\" across 5 sets at 70% 1RM (Cowley et al., 2025, *J Strength Cond Res* 39(4):e516–e529, randomised crossover, n=15). This is the strongest direct support for **downshifting load intra-session when performance is below expectation**. **[S]**\n- VBT's stated logic — build an individual load–velocity profile, measure warm-up/early-set velocity, and re-estimate the day's load from the divergence — is described but its *longitudinal* value is explicitly listed as an open question: \"Future research is, therefore, required to evaluate the longitudinal application of these approaches\" (Greig et al., 2020, *Sports Medicine*). **[R]**\n- Overall autoregulation vs fixed loading is a **small** advantage at best; the review's own summary is that these methods \"could be effective\", with small samples, few women, and Smith-machine dominance among the velocity studies (Larsen et al., 2021, *PeerJ*). Do not oversell. **[S]** for the caveat.\n\n### 5. Day-to-day variability in trained lifters\n\n- The widely quoted figure — daily 1RM fluctuating **±18%** (36% total range) — traces to Jovanović & Flanagan (2014, *J Aust Strength Cond*), a **review/commentary**, not an original measurement study. Cite it as an oft-repeated field estimate, never as a measured constant. **[X]** as a measured value; **[R]** as an order-of-magnitude field heuristic.\n- Defensible measured anchors instead: 1RM test–retest CV ranges 0.5–12.1%, median **4.2%**, ICC median 0.97 (Grgic et al., 2020, *Sports Med Open*, systematic review). **[S]**\n- Reps-to-failure–based 1RM estimation: ICC > 0.96, **CV 2.3–8.3%** across tests (Garcia-Ramos-group reliability work, 2019, *J Strength Cond Res* / 2023 *PLOS One*). **[S]**\n- Velocity at submaximal loads: intra-day CV 1.86–6.72%; intra-/inter-day reliability CV 2.4–9.7% and 3.2–8.6% respectively — i.e. **inter-day noise is of the same order as the weekly signal** (multiple LVP reliability studies, e.g. deadlift/hip-thrust LVP work, 2020–2026, *Appl Sci*, *J Strength Cond Res*). **[S]**\n- Direct reps-at-load figure: individual RIR–velocity relationships built in session 1 predicted session 2 (72 h later) with **mean error < 2 repetitions** in the back squat at 70/80/90% 1RM (Jukic et al., 2024, *Physiological Reports*, n=46 trained). So **±1–2 reps between sessions at the same load is normal** for a trained lifter. **[S]** — the single most useful number for a noise threshold.\n- Theoretical framing that matches this: \"readiness can be conceptualised as a stochastic fluctuation in performance with a mean equal to 0\", and \"adjustments to training generally need not be made unless the changes in performance differ from those that are expected as a result of the programme\" (Greig et al., 2020, *Sports Medicine*). **[S]** as a stated framework; **[R]** as a design rule.\n- Implication: **one below-par set inside a ±1–2 rep band is noise, not signal, and should move a prescription by approximately nothing.** **[R]**\n\n### 6. One prior session vs a 2–4 session rolling window\n\n- **No study has compared single-session vs rolling-window prescription bases.** This is a genuine evidence gap; any window choice is engineering, not science. **[X]**\n- The supporting argument is measurement-theoretic and rests on §5: with a between-session error of ~1–2 reps (Jukic et al., 2024, *Physiological Reports*) and 1RM CV median 4.2% (Grgic et al., 2020, *Sports Med Open*), a single session's estimate carries error comparable to a full week's expected progress, whereas averaging k sessions reduces the noise term by ~√k. **[R]**\n- Greig et al. (2020, *Sports Medicine*) supports the same conclusion qualitatively — adjust only when the deviation exceeds programme-expected change, and treat readiness as zero-mean noise. **[R]**\n- Counter-consideration: individual profiles *are* stable enough to be useful — individual (not general) RIR–velocity models had residual SE ~0.5–1.2 reps and \"displayed acceptable RSE, regardless of the load or testing session\" (Jukic et al., 2024). So personalised history is informative; it's the *single-observation* estimate that is fragile. **[S]**\n- Individual training responses are reproducible across two identical 10-week blocks (VL CSA r=0.697, biceps CSA r=0.761, leg press 1RM r=0.671; Räntilä et al., 2025, *Eur J Sport Sci*), which supports personalising rates of progression per user over time — but on a **block**, not session, timescale. **[S]**\n- Practical: a 2–4 session window with recency weighting is a **[R]** inference. Claiming it is \"more accurate\" as a proven fact is **[X]**.\n\n### 7. Set-position and exercise-order effects\n\n- Exercise order strongly affects *acute* work: total repetitions and volume are greater when an exercise is placed early in the session regardless of muscle mass involved; one study reported a multi-joint exercise losing **33.6%** of mean total reps and a single-joint exercise **42.3%** when moved later (Simão et al., 2012, *Sports Medicine* review; Miranda et al., 2013, *J Strength Cond Res*). **[S]**\n- Fatigue accumulates systematically across the session, not just within an exercise, and is reflected in rest-interval oxygen-consumption kinetics (Miranda et al., 2009, *J Sports Sci Med*). **[S]**\n- Long-term consequence is asymmetric: exercise order had **no** effect on hypertrophy (ES = 0.03, p = 0.862) and no overall effect on pooled strength (ES = −0.11, p = 0.306), but strength gains follow a specificity pattern favouring whatever is trained first (Nunes et al., 2021, *Eur J Sport Sci*, 11 studies, 268 participants). **[S]**\n- Not all populations show it — some studies found no order effect on reps-to-failure in younger/untrained samples (e.g. *J Strength Cond Res* untrained-male study, 2014). Order effects are **not universal**. **[S]**\n- **Is the individual's set-position decrement stable within-person? Not directly tested.** The nearest evidence is that fatigue response \"could have an individual response depending on the capacity of each subject to recover\" with large per-set CV (Heredia-Elvar et al., 2022, *Biology*), and that individual load–velocity/RIR profiles are stable across sessions (Jukic et al., 2024). Treating a *learned per-user, per-exercise* decrement as more reliable than a global constant is **[R]**; claiming it is a validated stable trait is **[X]**.\n\n### 8. What a whole-SESSION difficulty rating (1–5) can legitimately gate\n\n- Session-RPE is a validated construct for resistance training: it discriminates 90% / 70% / 50% 1RM intensities and is reliable (Day et al., 2004, *J Strength Cond Res*; Sweet et al., 2004, *J Strength Cond Res*), and 36 studies confirm validity/reliability of the session-RPE method across sports and both sexes (Haddad et al., 2017, *Front Neurosci*). **[S]**\n- Session-RPE was designed and validated as a **global internal-load monitoring** measure (intensity × duration), i.e. for tracking accumulated load and trends — **not** for reconstructing per-set proximity to failure (Foster et al., 2001, *J Strength Cond Res*; Haddad et al., 2017). **[S]**\n- Session-level perceptual measures **do** discriminate proximity-to-failure conditions over an 8-week intervention: post-session RPE and feeling-scale valence differed between failure and 1–2 RIR limbs (Refalo et al., 2025, *Eur J Sport Sci*, n=18 trained). So a session rating carries real information about how hard the whole session was. **[S]**\n- What per-set RIR gives that a session rating cannot: RIR accuracy is load- and proximity-dependent. Zourdos et al. (2016, *J Strength Cond Res*) found SD of reported RPE at 100/90/75/60% 1RM of **0.32 / 0.92 / 0.97 / 1.18** in experienced squatters, and velocity–RPE r = −0.88 (experienced) vs −0.77 (novice). Accuracy is a *within-set, near-failure* property. **[S]**\n- Estimation error grows away from failure: error ≈ **1 rep at 0–5 actual reps from failure** but **>2 reps at 7–10 reps from failure**; people under-predict by ~0.95 reps on average; chest press more accurate than leg press; training experience did **not** improve accuracy in that sample (Hackett et al., 2017, *J Strength Cond Res* / *PeerJ*). **[S]**\n- Therefore, legitimate uses of a 1–5 session rating: (a) a **global brake** — suppress or shrink load increases after a session rated maximally hard; (b) a **confirmation signal** — permit a larger increment when objective performance rose *and* the session was rated easy; (c) a **trend input** to a rolling readiness estimate. **[R]**\n- Illegitimate uses: inferring per-set RIR, inferring proximity to failure for any individual set, or scaling a specific load by a session rating. **[X]** — nothing in the literature maps a session-level score onto a set-level RIR.\n\n### 9. Proximity to failure — does +1 rep beat +load?\n\n- The definitive current meta-evidence: proximity to failure has **negligible** relationship with strength gain (CIs of the marginal slopes for estimated RIR contained the null in all best-fit models) but a **negative, non-null** slope for hypertrophy — muscle growth increases as sets terminate closer to failure (Robinson, Pelland, Remmert, Refalo, Jukic, Steele & Zourdos, 2024, *Sports Medicine* 54(9):2209–2231; 55 hypertrophy and 67 strength studies). **[S]**\n- The same authors explicitly caution that \"the exact relationship between RIR and muscle hypertrophy and strength remains unclear\" because the RIR values in the source studies were *estimated*, not directly controlled. Do not present the dose-response as precise. **[S]** for the caveat.\n- Direct RCT counterweight: 8 weeks, within-subject, 26 trained adults, failure vs 1–2 RIR produced **similar quadriceps hypertrophy**, with the failure limb reporting higher fatigue and RPE (Refalo et al., 2024, *J Sports Sci*). **[S]**\n- Combining §1 and §9: at matched effort, adding a rep at the same load and adding load at the same reps are **not meaningfully different for hypertrophy** (Plotkin et al., 2022, *PeerJ*), and load matters more for strength (Schoenfeld et al., 2017, *J Strength Cond Res*). **[S]**\n- What this does **not** license: claiming \"+1 rep beats +load\" or vice versa as a general rule. The honest statement is that both move the user closer to failure at the current capacity, and that is what the meta-regression identifies as the operative variable for hypertrophy. **[X]** for either directional claim.\n- Practical corollary the evidence *does* support: the resolver should protect **effort proximity** first (do not let a prescription drift into a chronically easy zone), and treat load-vs-reps as an equipment/goal question, not an efficacy question. **[R]**\n\n### 10. Practical equipment increments\n\n- **There is no peer-reviewed literature comparing load-increment granularity (e.g. 1 kg vs 2.5 kg vs 5 kg) for strength or hypertrophy outcomes.** Everything below is engineering/convention. **[X]** for any efficacy claim about increment size.\n- Barbell: standard smallest pair in most gyms is 1.25 kg plates → **2.5 kg minimum barbell increment**; many gyms stock only 2.5 kg plates → 5 kg minimum. Fractional plates (0.25–0.5 kg each, → 0.5–1 kg increments) exist but are user-supplied. Convention/product fact. **[R]**\n- Dumbbells: fixed racks typically step **2.0–2.5 kg per dumbbell** (5 lb in imperial gyms), which is **4–5 kg per bilateral movement** — often the largest relative jump a user faces. **[R]**\n- Selectorised machines / cable stacks: typical stack plates are **4.5–6.8 kg (10–15 lb)**, sometimes 20 lb, with optional add-on micro-plates (~1.1–2.3 kg) on some models. **[R]**\n- Relative-size problem: a 5 kg jump is 10% at 50 kg but 2.5% at 200 kg. Since ACSM's own recommendation is expressed as **2–10%** (ACSM, 2009), the smallest *available* increment on light exercises can already exceed the recommended band — meaning on light dumbbell/machine work, **rep progression is often the only increment inside the recommended range**. **[R]** — this is the strongest practical argument for a rep-first resolver on small lifts, and it follows from the equipment fact plus the ACSM band, not from a trial.\n- Design consequence: the resolver must know a per-exercise `minIncrement` and must never suggest an unachievable load. Rounding to the achievable grid is a correctness requirement, not a preference. **[R]**\n\n---\n\n## WHAT THIS LICENSES THE RESOLVER TO DO / FORBIDS IT FROM CLAIMING\n\n1. **LICENSED:** progress by reps *or* load interchangeably for hypertrophy — both are directly supported as equivalent (Plotkin 2022; IJSM 2024). Prefer load for stated strength goals (Schoenfeld 2017).\n2. **LICENSED:** trigger a load increase on a first/best-set criterion sustained over two sessions, using a 2–10% band rounded to the equipment grid (ACSM 2009). Call it a *convention*, not a proven rule.\n3. **LICENSED:** expect and pre-compensate set-to-set rep decay — larger on compounds, larger with short rest, larger nearer failure (Miranda 2009; Willardson 2005/2011; Heredia-Elvar 2022).\n4. **LICENSED:** adjust *later-set* targets from *earlier-set* performance today. This is precisely APRE's mechanism and has trial support (Mann 2010; Ghobadi 2022) plus acute mechanism support (Cowley 2025).\n5. **LICENSED:** treat a deviation of **±1–2 reps at a given load** as noise and make no prescription change (Jukic 2024; Greig 2020's zero-mean-readiness framing).\n6. **LICENSED:** base prescriptions on a recency-weighted 2–4 session window rather than the last session alone — as an explicitly *inferred* engineering choice justified by measurement error, not by a comparative trial.\n7. **LICENSED:** use the optional 1–5 session difficulty rating only as a **global brake or confirmation gate** on increment size and on stepping down after a maximally hard session (Day 2004; Haddad 2017; Refalo 2025).\n8. **FORBIDDEN:** inferring per-set RIR, per-set proximity to failure, or an e1RM adjustment from the session-level rating. No literature maps session-level ratings onto set-level RIR.\n9. **FORBIDDEN:** claiming double progression is evidence-based; claiming \"+1 rep beats +load\" (or the reverse); claiming an all-sets-at-top-of-range criterion is validated; claiming any specific increment size or rolling-window length is proven superior; quoting \"±18% daily 1RM variation\" as measured fact.\n10. **FORBIDDEN:** user-facing certainty language. The honest public framing is \"based on your recent sessions\" — never \"optimal\", never \"your true capacity is X\". Robinson et al. (2024) themselves state the RIR dose-response \"remains unclear\"; the resolver must not be more confident than its sources.\n\n**Two flags for the design doc.** (a) Sex moderates within-session decrement — women needed ~10% vs men ~15% load reduction to hold reps across squat sets (Willardson group) — but n is small and this should be treated as a hypothesis, not a coefficient. (b) A large-scale preprint (arXiv 2603.17495, \"A Weight-Dependent 1RM Prediction Equation Optimized on 303,494 Near-Failure Sets\") repeatedly surfaced as a candidate e1RM formula source; it is **not peer-reviewed and I did not verify it**. Do not adopt it without a separate review pass.\n\nSources: [Plotkin 2022 PeerJ](https://peerj.com/articles/14142/) · [IJSM 2024 overload
